My dear Secretary Hull:--
Depertsment of State
I thank you for your letter of May 22nd
in regard to the export of war materials to Japan.
It states the question clearly and, it seems to me,
894.24/668
ought to be satisfactory to the people who are so
greatly disturbed about the shipment of arms and
other war supplies to Japan. I receive letters
every day from Kansas people who are greatly con-
cerned over this situation. I can make good use
of about 25 additional copies of the mimeographed
statement which you sent me in your letter of May 22.
Sincerely yours,
Anthun Tapper

I am in receipt of a petition, signed
by a number of residents of Farmington, Michigan,
opposing the shipment of goods to Japan that con-
tribute toward the maintaining of her war against
China.
I shall appreciate a letter from you,
advising me of the present attitude of the Depart-
ment upon this matter.

courteous attention, I am

your influence toward immediate Action. We ask the boycott of all
goods or material whatsoever to Japan that contributes to maintain-
Ing her WAR against China, until such War shall cease.

WASHINGTON COMMITTEE FOR AID TO CHINA
Room 203
1115-15th Street, N.W.
Washington, D. C.
Republic 0865
May 1, 1939
The Honorable
The Secretary of State:
Sir,
The Washington Committee for Aia to China
transmits herewith to you a petition signed by six
thousand five hunared signatories appealing for Ameri-
can action against Japanese aggression within the
framework of existing laws. At the time of the
signature of this petition, such action seemed to
present the best possibility of halting the Japanese
assault on China; since then, however, progress on the
legislative front has been considerable and the same
conditions ao not now hold.
Under the new conditions, though, the main argument
of the petitioners is still pertinent. Sixty five hundred
people still signify their aesire that the Departnent of
State lead the way in determining America's attitude towards
the struggle in the Orient. We present the petition, there-
fore, in the hope that it will strengthen your hand in the
matter of assuring that that attitude shall be an honorable
one and effective toward peace.
Very truly yours,
MORTIMER GRAVES
Chairman

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.97)
DCR
STAT
DIVISION OF
BECK
MAY
1939
ULYARI
PA/E
This morning I took the attached file
to Mr. Welles. He read your memorandum of
May 1. Thereupon he said that in the case
of the American citizens at Chengtu, whose
message the Department communicated to the
House Committee on Foreign Affairs and the
Senate Committee on Foreign Relations,
these Americans were residing abroad and
had no ready means of communicating with
members of Congress. He said also that in
the present case he felt strongly that the
Department should not undertake to act as
an agency of transmission to members or
committees of Congress; that American citi-
zens or organizations in this country,
especialiy those in Washington, were in a
position to communicate their views directly
to members of Congress or committees of
Congress. He repeated that he felt strongly
that in cases such as this the Department
should not act as an agency of transmission.
It seems to me that no further action
need be taken in regard to the attached
file other than perhaps to bring it to the
attention of the Secretary as a matter of
information.
3.3.H.
C

May 1, 1939.
8 1939
Petition by Washington Committee for Aid to China
June
The letter here attached and the petition referred
F.W.894.24/673
to therein were handed to me today by Dr. Frederick Brown
Harris, Pastor of the Foundry M.E. Church of washington,
F.W.
D.C., in the presence of and on behalf of a delegation
representing the ashington Committee for Aid to China.
The letter is signed by Mr. Mortimer Graves,
894.24/673
chairman.
The letter states that the petition is "signed by
six thousand five hundred signatories appealing for Ameri-
894.24
can action against Japanese aggression within the frame-
work of existing laws."
The petition reads: "Feeling that the people of the
United states are morally responsible for the slaughter of
millions of people as long as they allow the shipment of
C.
C
materials necessary for the pursuit of war such as oil,
scrap iron, cotton, timber, etc., we call upon our President
F/WB
and the Department of State to find some way under existing
legislation to prohibit such snipments to Japan."
The letter states that since the time at which the
petition was signed "progress on the legislative front has
been considerable" but that "Under the new conditions, . .
the

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
the main argument of the petitioners is still pertinent"
and "Sixty five hundred people still signify their desire
that the Department of State lead the way in determining
America's attitude towards the struggle in the Orient."
Dr. Harris stated that the ashington Committee for
Aid to China approve of and support the attitude and
policies of the Secretary of State and wish to strengthen
his hanas.
In accepting the petition and replying to Dr. Harris'
remarks, I made the observation that the foundation and
background of foreign policy in this country is public
opinion; that the Administration welcomes expressions of
opinion and formulates policies in the light of the best
indices which it can obtain of the wishes of the nation;
and that in many connections action taken by the executive
branch of the Governnent depends upon authorization and
provision given or withheld by the legislative branch.
Dr. Harris indicated that the petitioners would like
to have their petition brought to the attention of appro-
priate members of the Congress. I said that I would bring
the petition to the attention of the Secretary of State
and would see what further disposal might be made of it.
工

-3 -

the contrary copies of Mr. Graves' letter and of this
memorandum be sent to the Chairman of the Senate Com-
mittee on Foreign Relations and to the Chairman of the
House Committee on Forein Affairs.
Stanley K. Hornbeck

erme, ammunition, and iuploments of war. The artioles and

Dear Sir:
Department of State
As the latest protest of our
r government against Japanese bombing of
China's cities, one is inescapably intrigued by a recent situation
894.2
that would be comic if it were not so grim - our navy going back to
the Pacific to help England keep an eye on Japan, when suddenly we
have the specticle of an English freighter going aground off the
coast of Washington, laden with scrap-iron for Japan which had been
purchased in the United States.
Mr. Hull, we who have followed your career as a statesman have the
greatest admiration and respect for your diplomatic achievements, and,
naturally your opinions. Please, why must our nation risk involve-
ment in war in the Orient by protecting our investments there - and
England's tin and rubber trade? The people of America sickened of
the Panay incident when they found that the boat was in the question-
able company of some Standard il tankers. Why do we not remove our
military forces from China and let the "open door" slam in some other
face? China didn't appreciate the way we opened the door in the first
place. Why do we continue to support the dictatorship in Cuba against
the will of the people there? In the face of this what right have we
to dondemn dictatorships elsewhere?
As patriotic American citizens with American sense of justice and an
innate desire that our Country stand aloof from imperialistic tenden-
cies and "dollar diplomacy" and maintain a program of constructive co-
operation with other less fortunate nations in peaceful negotiations
we cannot feel kindly toward these discrepancies.
Very truly yours,
(Mrs. W. S. Lorimer)

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
1osure no. 2 to despatch no. 108, dated May 23, 1939,
m Alvin T. Rowe, Jr., American Vice Consul at Foochow,
hina, on the subject of "Editorial in Foochow paper:
American responsibility for continued aggression in
China".
English translation of an editorial appearing in Nan
Fang Jih Pao, on May 13, 1939.
CRIMES FOR WHICH THE UNITED STATES
SHOULD SHARE THE RESPONSIBILITY
In view of the wanton air raids staged by Japanese
planes which resulted in the tragic loss of life and
property to foreign residents, Americans at Chungking
have cabled to the United States Government, requesting
that prompt action be taken to prohibit the export of
munitions of war and military supplies to the aggressor.
The telegram reads in brief as follows:
"Chungking Americans are aghast at the
mass murder and destruction of the Japanese
raids here. The unspeakable tragedy and suf-
fering will only increase China's determina-
tion to resist, which is heroic and unshaken.
We protest at the so-called neutrality, which
permits the sale of gas, oil, metals, cotton
and machinery, which for twenty-two months
has made the United States a party to Japan's
inhuman crimes.
"We appeal in God's name for immediate
action in placing an embargo on traffic of
all commodities used by the aggressors in
waging war."*
This open-hearted appeal that Chungking Americans
made to their Government is, on the one hand, worth
our sympathy and, on the other, increasing our suspicion
that America's course of action assists the wicked.
The United States has hitherto taken interest in China
and shown its sympathy. We the Chinese people have
also for a long time regarded the United States as our
good friend. It is without doubt, however, continuing
to assist our cruel enemy to invade China. Such an
act
*Exact quotation of English version taken from South
China Morning Post, dated May 1l, 1939.

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
act admits of no explanation so far as friendship is
concerned, nor does it admit of any so far as justice
and righteousness are concerned; there is still less
ground to admit of any explanation from the standpoint
of the maintenance of world peace.
Not long ago, in a peace conference convened at
Paris it was decided that munitions of war and all
accessories for use in air raids should be forbidden
to be supplied to an aggressor. Was not t at decision
most appropriate and dignified as an unchangeable law?
Up to the present, let me ask, what country and indi-
vidual among the world powers has put it into force?
Capable and enterprising as President Roosevelt is,
he appears to be earnest in remonstrance but too soft-
hearted. Everyday he speaks of the sup ort of justice
and the control of brute force. Yet he fails to put
his words into action and to set himself up as an
example. The attitude of others is an easy matter of
conjecture.
Public opinion in the United States has been
increasingly critical of the Governnent for quite
sometime. An editorial from a Washington newspaper
states:
"The people are opposed to the sale of
munitions of war to Japan for her conquest
of China. Aside from supplying Japan with
munitions which enable her to destroy American
properties in China and the lives of American
women and children, we have also allowed
ourselves to sink to a position where we
are at the mercy of the Japanese."
The New York Post says:
"We need not deceive ourselves. The
scheme mapped out for the expansion of our
armaments in the army and navy is beyond question
designed

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
designed to put us in readiness to resist
any Japanese attack; in that case, then
why should we assist Japan to conquer China
and thus pave the way for the steady growth
of its power? Moreover, what is our real
motive in continuing traffic with Japan
in articles such as are required by our own
army and navy, to the detriment of our national
defense project?"
Another American newspaper makes the following
editorial comment:
"The United States must be held partly
responsible (for Japanese outrages] because,
though Japan actually commits the acts of
aggression and brutality itself, the weapons
which it relies upon for massacre and des-
truction and invading another nation are
manufactured by our own hands."
These stern and just remarks are fully in agree-
ment with the telegram of appeal which Chungking
Americans despatched. Does it mean to say that
President Roosevelt will always be deaf to these
important events as if his ears were stopped? Admit-
ting that he might not act through sympathy with
China, does it mean to say that he will disregard the
life and property of American residents and the equal
opportunity of Americans in China?
The enemy planes have carried out air raids on an
American gunboat; the bombs used were made of materials
supplied by the United States. They carried out air
raids on American citizens: the bombs used were also
made of materials supplied by the United States. They
carried out air raids on American properties; and
materials used in the bombs were likewise supplied by
the United States. This is, in fact, the meaning of
the saying, "To let your spears be used against your
own shield." The United States has not only been
swindled

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
swindled, but it has been committing suicide. We are
at a loss to understand why the United States should
go out of its way to do such a supremely foolish thing.
The physical appearance of our cruel enemies is
hideous: it is futile for us to blame them for the
lowest forms of vice and the display of their animal
instincts. What arouses most suspicion in us is the
inconsistent attitude of the United States. Her mouth
clamors for peace, while her hands ar engaged in assist-
ing the war. She has exhibited her sympathy towards
human kind, international justice and righteousness in
such a way that at times it may be visible but at
other times it is not, and [her attitude] is now apparent
and again ambiguous. We are wrapped in mystery; we can-
not but sigh with regret.
The Americans in Chungking state frankly:
"The
United States is a party to Japan's inhuman crimes."
Let it be hoped that all the people of the United
States, the leaders and the governed of all classes,
may repeat these words three times!
Trans. WYT
Copied HCY

Re: Japanese Aggression
Dear Representative McCormack:
From authentic sources I find that Japan could not
carry on her war against China but a short time if she
could not receive war materials and credit from the
United States.
This being the case may I strongly urge that you use
every legitimate means available to secure legislation
which will stop supplying such equipment.
Thanking you in advance, I am
Very respectfully,
DUDLEY L. HIGGINS,
Town Treasurer.

Under date of June 27th, I have received a comnuni-
894.24/678
cation from Mr. C. P. lthaus, Editor, The Bethel Journal, of
Bethel, Ohio, in which he has enclosed an editorial from the
June 23d issue of the Journal entitled "A Suicidal American
Policy".
Mr. Althaus is unusually well acquainted with the

fourteen years, and I believe that in the last paragraph of
his editorial is contained a most logical solution of this
894124
troublesome situation.
In this connection, I wish also to respectfully call

of the Christian Science Monitor for Tuesday, June 27th, en-
titled " U. S. Chief Arms Provider to Japan", together with a
table showing how the United States aids Japan.
It seems to me that these conditions should be tho-
roughly investigated by the State Department with the view of
working out some sort of mutual agreement with England and France,
and probably other nations, to the end thet Japanese aggressions
against American property will be stopped.

SCRIPTURE: "The Heavens declare the glory of God, and
the firmamont showeth His handiwork." Ps. 19:1.
PRAYER: "Lord of life beneath the dome, of the universe
Thy home; gather us who seek Thy face, to the fold of Thy em-
brace, for Thou art nigh." Amen.
A SUICIDAL AMERICAN POLICY
Daily the situation in the Far East seems to grow more
critical. England and Japan have reached a point where it is
becoming increasingly difficult for either to back down with-
out much loss of face. England is being accused of openly
espousing the cause of Chiang Kai Chek, ruler of China. Japan
is insisting that she fall in line with the "new order of things",
thus recognizing Japanese control. Never has the proud
Briton dealt with a delicate situation so patiently and accepted
openly such personal insults, as the Japanese are heaping upon
him daily. A decade ago His Majesty's Navy would have
steamed quickly to the Far East to settle such matters and en-
tirely to the satisfaction of England. Now the situation has
vastly changed. Hitler and Mussolini are troubling her in
Enrope making it inadvisable to leave European waters.
For the present Japan is dealing gently with Americans
in the concessions. She wants the good-will of our nationals for
a very definite purpose. If she can bring England, the strong-
est power in the Far East, to her knees, the other nations will
quickly have to follow. The Open Door policy will then be
scrapped in double quick time. American and British trade
will be swallowed up in China by the Nipponese, just as they
have done in Manchuko. Western powers have reached the
cross roads as far as trade in China is concerned.
There is another angle to the situation which has a close
bearing upon the situation. The Japanese army is mired in
the far interior of China and is mraking no progress. Japan is
sincerely wishing she were out of the China mess. To save
Japanese face, the navy—always at loggerheads with the
army—wants to get in action by taking the several rich foreign
concessions along the coast, which could be siezed. She would
gladly stop her warfare if she could get away with these rich
prizes. America, England and France would in effect be
paying for the Japanese blunder in China, if the concessions
are taken.
American interest in China has been far greater than
merely commercial. Various Mission Boards have established
Medical Schools, Colleges, Theological Schools, Hospitals,
High Schools and Churches thruout that land, with property
values amounting to millions of dollars. Recent reports state
that more than 20 American missions have been bombed in the
last few months. This is being considered as an organized
Japanese campaign to eradicate foreign missions from China.
The Japanese see in these institutions powerful factors exert-
ing a steadying influence over the Chinese and weaning them
away from cooperating with the Nippons.
In this struggle America ought to go along closely with
England and France. If these three nations would bring
economic pressure to bear, selling no iron ore, tin, copper and
asoline to Japan, the latter would feel the pinch in a few
hort weeks. Little else would need to be done. Our present
neutrality law is faulty on this score and needs to be amend-
ed. Will we stupidly continue to furnish Japan with the
weapons she will soon use against us? Our present policy is
absolutely suicidal.

The Honorable Arthur Capper

Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Capper:
We, the Committee of the Kansas Delegation at the Annual
Southwide Camp of Young Woman's Auxiliaries of Southern
Baptist Convention, desire to present to you our sentiments
concerning the present Japanese war situation, and the
participation of our country in its continuance.
We ask that you, our representative in the Senate, shall
do all you can to further such legislation as will end all
shipping of raw, or finished, war materials to Japan.
One thousand girls here at camp, from eighteen states,
representing 77,143 Baptist young women in our organizations,
have enthusiastically endorsed a boycott of all Japanese
goods, including silk hose and other silk products, upon
the sale of which Japan depends, in part, for means to carry
on her undeclared war with China. We believe that this
boycott is a genuinely friendly act toward the people of
Japan who are suffering from the burdens of war.
We are deeply interested, and heartily enlisted, in the
ralsing of relief money for China. We have come to know
that the suffering in China is caused by American-bought
bombs, carried by American-made airplanes, and that the
Japanese armies have been armed largely with machine guns
and other war equipment made in America. This situation
outrages both our sense of consistenoy and of justice. We
appeal to you, as our representative, to do all that you
can to end the participation of our American munition
manufacturers in this war with China. Is it possible to
enaot

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
enact legislation which shall end the shipping of sorap
iron to Japan?
We are deeply interested in the problem of the Neutrality
Act for the United States. It seems to us that the Schwellen-
back Bill, unless a better one is introduced, should be
passed, and we would greatly appreciate your support of this
Neutrality Act.
We would be very happy to hear from you concerning this
matter, and will give your reply wide circulation among
the Kansas Baptist Churches and our press in the further
marshalling of public opinion in this vital matter.
Very truly yours,
UNA ROBERTS LAWRENCE Ch.

Mr. Moffett referred particularly to an article which ap-

any proclamation which he might issue under section 1 of
the present neutrality act.

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.98)
 U. S. Continues to Supply More than Half of Japan's Imports
of War Materiale
During 1938, Japan obtained approximately 56 per cent of her imported
materials of war from the United States -- a proportion slightly higher than the
famous 54 per cent of 1937. The most important Amerioan war sales to Japan and
"Manohukuo" in 1938 were petroleum and petroleum products ($53 million), iron
materials inoluding sorap iron ($35 million), metal working machinery ($24 million),
copper (22 million), airoraft and parts ($17 million), automobilen and parts and
accessorios (12 million). The volume of war exports to Japan has been maintained
during the early wonths of 1939.
PENDING BILLS AND RESOLUTIONS AFFECTING THE FAR EAST
1. - B. J. Res. 123, introduoed by Senator Key Pittman (Dem.), Chairman of the
Foreign Relations Committee, would authorize the President to place restrio-
tions upon trade - imports and exports (except agrioultural) - and upon oredits
between the United States and any nation whioh, through violation of the Nine-
Power Paot, endangers the lives of our oitizens or deprives them of their
logal rights and privileges under the terms of this treaty. Seotion 3 of the
resolution provides that restriotions are not to be applied until ten days
after the President has submitted to Congress, while in session, the proolama-
tion whioh he intends to make relative to such restriotions.
2. - B. J. Res. li3, introduced by Senator Lewis B. Schwellenbach (Dem.) - also
introduoed in the House as H. J. Res. 318 by Congressman M. C. Wallgren (Dem.)
would authorize the President to withhold from export all materials (exoept
agrioultural produots), which there is reason to believe will be used to vio-
late the sovereignty of any nation whose "sovereignty, independenoe, or terri-
torial or administrative integrity" the United States is obligated by treaty to
respeot. This also would be applioable only under the Nine-Power Paot.
3. - H. R. 6837, introduced by Congressman Charles A. Eaton (Rep.), whioh would
authorize the President, after proolamation, to prohibit export of any artiole
or commodity whioh there is reason to believe will be used in violation of any
treaty whereby any nation has agreed with the United States to respeot "the
sovereignty, the independenoe, and the territorial and administrative in-
tegrity" of any other nation. Congress is empowered to reverse suoh aotion
by oonourrent resolution. This would be applioable under the Nine-Power Paot.
4. - H. R. 643&, introduoed by Congressman John M. Coffee (Dem.), whioh would pro-
hibit, by aot of Congress, the supplying of arms, ammunition, and implements
and materials of war, as well as war oredits, to Japan until her armed foroes
are withdrawn from China. Materials of war, as defined, inolude metals,
potroleum, maohinery: not ootton.

The Honorable Franklin D. Roosevelt
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. President:
May I submit for your consideration a partial list of organizations
national, regional, and local -- which have protested against the continued sale
894.24/684
of war supplies to Japan for her war of aggression against Chira?
A conservative estimate, allowing for duplications, indicates that
nearly 40 million cit zens are represented by these active protests.
In some cases, the organizations cited decried help to aggressors
generally; most of the actions listed, however, dealt specifically with the ex-
port of war materials to Japan. I believe that in practically every case, there
has been an outright request for governmental action, with the exception of the
protest issued by the Federal Council of Churches which is addressed primarily to
private manufacturers or merchants. The list does not take account of a very large
number of individual petitions, through letters and resolutions.
All of these, together with the recent Gallup poll, furnish unmistake-
able evidence, we believe, of the mounting tide of feeling among a large majority
of the American people against continuing any longer the position of the United
FILED
States as Japan's chief support for the ruthless invasion of China.
This is sent to you with the knowledge that no one else can more fully
appreciate its significance in relation to pending legislation.
()
9
With high regards, I am
CASTLAN AFFAIRS
Very sincerely yours,
P

THE INDIANAPOLIS TIMES

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
347 Reamer Place
Oberlin, Ohio
July 20, 1939.
The Honorable Dow Harter,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Congressman:
As the adjournment of Congress grows near, I am
increasingly distressed by the possibility that nothing
may be done to stop our arming of Japan for 1ts brutal
assault upon China.
I have been in both countries and write from broad
knowledge and with strong conviction. No interests are
more urgent and critical for the United States. As one
of your constituency, I believe I have a right to urge
you to insist upon action now. Congress must not leave
this condition as it is; you must know how general the
popular resentment is.
Respectfully yours,
(Miss) FLORENCE M. FITCH
Professor Oberlin College

Honorable Cordell Hull

Departmentof State
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Washington, D. C.
My dear Secretary Hull:
Department of State
894.24/688
I am writing to ask if there is not something you can do to stop the slaughtering
of the Chinese and civilians by the Japanese. As you will note by the heading on
this letter paper, I am Treasurer of the Woman's Christian Medical College, Shanghai,
China.
In November l937, the Japanese entered; we had to evacuate, removing all our patiants
as well as the staff to other quarters. We have not yet been able to use our
buildings and I am wondering what our best act with regard to same should be.
You know very well that if the United States Government would stop all munitions
and credit to Japan, this slaughtering would cease. We read in the Book upon which
all oaths are taken that the 'Sins of the fathers shall be visited upon the children'
and if these munitions manufacturers in the United States and the capitalists are
using this method to gain in wealth, God help their children in the future.
You are the one man in Washington who can do something about this if you wish, and
I am writing to urge that you give this your serious, Christian consideration. Please
embargo immediately all munitions and credit to Japan. I believe that some relation-
ship between Japan and China similar to the United States and Canada will be the
solution of these troubles. Can you not, in your diplomatic way, do something toward
drawing a line between the two Nations and bring Peace among them?
Sincerely yours

My dear Mr. Secretary:

Several years ago  had the pleasure of
meeting you at the time when we were both receiving
degrees at Yale. Since then I have hoped that there
would he an opportunity for me to call on you some
time in Washington, but I find myself still looking
forward to this pleasure.
I now write you in re-
gard to the following matter, because I feel that as
8
a citizen of this country I must express both to you
6
and to the members of Congress,my conviction in regard
94.24/69
to our relationship with Japan. I feel sure trat you
sympathize with the desire of meny citizens of this
country to see our present traffic in war material
with Jupan cut off. I,therefore, wish to express my
894.24
conviction that the vast majority of our people are
backing you in your convicticn in this matter.
6
O
During recent weeks I have traveled quite
extensively in various parts of the country east of
the Mississippi. I have been in both New England,
along the Atlantic Seaboard, the South,and here in
the Middle West. I find people everywhere more and
more shocked by the fact that well over half of the
war materials being used by Japan comes from the
United States. Our willingness to carry on this
traffic in essential war materials with a country which
has violated its sacred obligations with our own country,
J0L  871939
and is carrying on a shameful war of aggression without
F
FTLED
concern for any laws of decency or morality, should not
A1
be longer permitted or endured.
If the United States continues to make it
possible for Japan to carry on its present warfare
against China, I believe that this country will stand
in history as one of the most hypocritical and selfish
examples in the whole field of international relation-
ship.
I have discussed this matter many tires with
large groups of people, both here in this diocese,where
the members of the Episcopal Church are deeply concerned,

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.97)
and elsewhere, as I have had a chance to meet with in-

At our diocesan convention, made up of

and m ssions in this Liocese of Southern Ohio, the
matter was thoroushly discussed an a vote taken
resulting inone hundred per cent appeal to Congress
asking for legislation which would do everything pos-
sible to stop the trade with Japan.
In view of all this, both on my own behalf,
and on behalf of the members of the Church in this
diocese, and many others with whon: I have talked, I
urge that you as Secretary of State, and the members
of Congress do everything possible to take some action
at the present session which will correct the existing
intollerable situation.

My dear Secretary Hull:
I have received today a letter and document from Joseph C. Green, Chiof of Division of
Controls with regard to my letter to you of June 27th regarding the s tuathlon?in China.
"There are no statutes now in existence authorizing the placing of ogtrictions
RUH/EMU
8
defined as arms, ammunition, and implements of war, tin-plate scrap or
394.24/69
helium gas. Before the export to any country of other manufactured
products, or of raw materials, such as scrap iron, could be prohibited, new
legislation would be required."
Also in his letter, he states that,
"The Neutrality Act of May 1, 1937 provides that the President shall,
whenever he finds that a state of war exists between or among two or
6
more foreign countries, proclaim such fact, that thereafter the export
of arms, ammunition, and implements of war to both parties to the conflict
shall be prohibited."
I presume you are aware of the fact that Japan plans to bomb all educational centers because
in these centers are found the leaders of China. I wish to quote herein from some of the
reports that have come to us regarding these bombings. From a member of the faculty of the
West China Union University, Chengtu, June 12, 1939 - received in New York June 23rd, as
follows:
"Yesterday evening at about 7:20 we heard a great roar of Chinese pursuit.
planes in the sky, little realizing that something had gone wrong with the
warning siren and these were climbing to meet Japanese bombers. We had
just finished supper when suddenly there were a terrific series of explosions,
and I realized that a raid was on. I dashed to a previously selected spot
in the house and threw myself on the floor, as there was no time to get to
the dugout. Almost immediately there was a burst of flame and dust accompanied
by a deafening explosion, which sent glass and plaster flying about me. I
thought our house had been hit. Pausing a moment to make sure there would
be no more bombs till the planes had a chance to turn, I dashed in search of
my wife, and then with her and other friends made for the dugout. Two of these
had gotten up out of sick beds. When it seemed safe to do so, I set out to
FILED
learn what had happened to others on our Baptist row. Except for my wife who
G
had a splinter of glass narrowly miss one eye which made a minor cut on her
8
forehead and the servant of our next door neighbors who had a more serious
wound, the only damage had beento our buildings.
.All of them had
9
3
suffered a measure of damage, several including ours with windows and doors
9
wrenched from hinges and littered with plaster and glass. At first I could not
make out where the bomb which had done all this damage had landed, but presently
the spot was located across the tennis courts at a corner of our Baptist middle
school dormitory. The wing had been largely demolished. Apparently it had been
intended for the large new hospital buildings just behond, but had fallen a

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
/59
-2-
CH
"bit short. Two women servants were injured, but no one fatally. The dormitory is
used by the refugee professors of National Central Medical College of Nanking. They
are crowded a whole family to one or two rooms. The bomb had actually exploded in
the air through hitting a large tree. This explained the wide area affected. Pieces
of the shrapnel entered the Dye, Moncrieff, and our houses, one being found this
morning by the desk at which Mrs. Dye usually sits. At the Moncrieff house shrapnel
had been driven through several walls.
"While calling at the dormitory I learned from a physician friend of the killing of
Miss Huang Hsiao-cho, a student in West China Union University, a lovely girl who was
studying to be a pharmacologist. Later I learned that she had been struck in the
head by a missile and killed almost instantly.
"This morning we were enabled to appreciate how extremely fortunate we had been as
a university community, for out of the six bombs dropped on us three were duds and
one did not land near buildings. After the 26 attacking planes had left a wake of
destruction across the city, part of the squadron at least made straight for our
campus. The first bomb exploded on the river bank and in the water. It was the one
which did the most damage, wrecking a good part of the house now occupied by President
Chen of Nanking University, and doing much destruction also to that occupied by Dr.
Liljestrand. Both these are Methodist residences. Mrs. Iiljestrand was injured on
arm and breast by glass. President Chen who had started downstairs was hurled in
a somersault to the bottom and landed on his back. He suffered considerable injury
and will be laid up for at least some days. His sister, Miss Mary Chen, who is also
on the faculty, suffered a deep scalp wound and is receiving tetanus inoculation.
Hismother also suffered injury - how serious I am not informed. The second bomb
landed just to the rear of the house occupied hitherto by Dr. and Mrs. Lewis of the
Methodist Mission. Fortunately it was a dud. If either this bomb had exploded or
the first one had fallen ten yards further, the missionary residence portion of the
Methodist compound along with the occupants, none of whom had had time to take
refuge in dugouts, would have been practically obliterated.
"Two other bombs, both duds, landed one on either side of the University library.
Had they exploded not only would this splendid building have been destroyed, but
the recently erected temporary buildings of the Ginling Women's College along
with their occupants. I am not clear as to location of the sixth bomb. Except for
it all bombs fell immediately adjacent to large buildings.
"Part of the University Administration building was turned over for use as emergency
hospital, mangled forms of men, women and children being brought in and laid on straw
throughout first part of the night. During the night six died, and several others
are so injured that they will also probably die. Physicians and students of the joint
universities rendered splendid service in caring for these.
"Those of us trying to carry on here under these conditions are curious to know how
much longer Americe is going to aid and abet Japan's bombing atrocities by supplying
her with the sinews of war. We have long been aware over here that it is such assis-
tance which protracts the war, and that the planes that came here were powered by
American oils and gas, and the bombs dropped were made in large part from Americen
scrap iron. Can selfish fear of angering Japan atone for such guilt?"
Another report received in New York, June 29th, 1939 is as follows:
"You will know something of what has happened, perhaps a good deal, but I want to
tell you of our end of this story. Last night they came, twemty-five or six planes.
Chengtu is largely defenseless - no caves like Chungking which is built on solid
rock - water just a little below the surfact of the land, so good dugouts are
impossible. We have them, but they do not provide much safety. Suddenly the enemy
air planes were on us and though our dugouts were very near, I doubt if any one got
there before the bombing was over. Five bombs fell on this campus, ruining two
residences, and hitting a dormitory of the Central University fauilty, or rather,
shaking it nearly to pieces. The President of Nanking University and his family had

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
CH
worst family experience, with nearly everyone injured to some extent. The house
 Dr. and Mrs. Liljestrand, also was shaken badly by bombs falling very near. Mrs.
Liljestrand was slightly injured. The old medical buildings had a corner torn off,
the building used by the University of Nanking for offices and classrooms had all doors
and windows blown in, and one corner of our gymnasium was injured. Two bombs, which
failed to explode, fell very near us, and if either one had gone off, perhaps we
would not be here now, or many would have been injured, and our dormitory down."
This gives you some idea of what is really going on at University quarters. I am wondering
just what in needed to convince President Roosevelt that a state of war exists in China
by the aggressive bombing by the Japanese. Perhaps if this were happening at our
university centers in the United States, the President might then be convinced that a
state of war existed. You with the President have the power of stopping the sale of
war material like scrap iron, oil and cotton to Japan and we here in America are looking
to you for prompt action in this matter. I certainly hope that if the President does
not act, the matter will be taken in hand by our Congressmen and Senators as they have
taken other matters in hand when the President has failed us.
With regard to our applying to the American Consul General in Shanghai whom you state
is prepared to render all appropriate assistance with regard to our Shanghai property
which was looted and distroyed by the Japanese as far back as November 1937, may I say
that so long as the President of the United States does not consider a state of war
exists, what use would it be for us to claim any assistance with regard to the actions
against the Japanese in Shanghai? You may be interested to know that should we now
take action when (?) no state of war exists, we are liable to be singled out by the
Japanese and our American nurses and doctors become the victims of further bombings.
An American hospital means nothing to the Japanese as they are singling out the
hospitals as well as the universities for distruction. One of our missionaries who
had helped the Chinese was followed by a Japanese all the way to Manila as she left
China for her furlough.
Perhaps we know a little bit more about what is taking place actually then some of these
officials in Washington who scan reports and as no war has been declared existent by
the President, the reports are tabled.
We now come to you, asking immediate action. If the United States would stop sending
scrap iron, oil and cotton to Japan this murderous slaughtering of innocents would
stop within a few months. Please remember it is American Property that is being
destroyed as well as lives.
Awaiting prompt action by you, I am,
Sincerely yours
Gduilen
Hilda L. Olson, Asst. Treasurer

The Right Hon. Sir Franklin D. Roosevelt,
The President of the United States of Americe
White House, Washington,
U. S. A.
Dear Sir:
America is the nation that loves peace and
hates war ! The golden words "freedom, equality and fra-
ternity" make the great spirit of the Democracy of the
United States of America. In the Far East, however, there
is also a peace-loving nation which faces, unfortunately.
a struggle for maintaining its national existence, and in-
494.24
dependent sovereignty, and which has sacrificed more than
hundreds of thousands lives for saving the justice out of
the barbaric militarism. This is the Republic of China!
Every sympathetic aid to China to stop this sanguinary war
is cordially welcomed. China demands Justice! Peace with
honour is needed!
Dr. Homer P. Rainey recently summed up the praise-
worthy development in the United States of the finest system
of public education that any society has ever known. Educa-
tion founded on morality encourages one to give up his sel-
8
fish mind in spite of all difficulties. In the civilized
nation, a well-educated man hates war. In the same way, a
6
4.2
well-edueated merchant would heartily refuse to export the
weapons of death to the aggressors. The sad news we are incee
ssantly receiving about some one providing day and night the
4/69
Japanese imperialists with all things necessary for invading
China has greatly excited the whole world. And the trade-
mark of the "eggs" thrown down by Japanese in. the bombard-
ment of defenseless cities in China reads "Made in the Uni-
2
ted States of America". Money can be the only sweetheart
of such merchants, who are despising the lives of others
and even themselves, simultaneously. I am sure that the
educated American merchants would never commit such in-D
human  cts, I hope the ill news spread by foreign obser
vers in China are not true. In case, there still are trose
who enchanted by money, furnish munitions, aircrafts and
ED
the like to an aggressive country, I sincerely pray that
9
your Honour will soon prohibit their export.
3
In order to secure the real welfare of human bAngs,
the peace of the world is necessary. Humanity first! And
then the so-called "Security" naturally follows. Let us
think over a while, whether "Right makes Might" or "Might
F
makes Right".

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
The contemporary "Isolation lolicy" is unable to
cope with the present emergency. A policy based on mora
lity will certainly make for mutual understanding betwan
nations. I know no one at the present moment who wants
war, unless it be those who are ambitious militarists
---- aggressors.
In a word, a God-controlled world may engender the
real pacific and riglit means to solve any disputes in the
family of nations.
All brothers in the world!
One God only!
I have the honour to be,
with the hi hest consideration,
Sir,
Your most obedient,
humble servant,
Lincoln Y. Wang,
A citizen of China.
Shanghai China.

Miss Fabyan stated that her delegation represented
the Boston Branoh of the American Committee for Non-
Partioipation in Japanese Aggression. On their way to
894.24/693
Washington they had, during the course of their passage
through New England, gathered thousands of names to a
petition favoring the enactment of the Pittman Resolution
whioh authorizes an embargo on the export of certain
commodities to Japan. Miss Fabyan said that they had come
to Washington to advocate the passage of the Resolution
and inquired how they could most effeotively achieve
their purpose. Mr. Sayre suggested that the question of
the placing of an embargo on exports to Japan was a
matter for Congress to decide and suggested that the
delegation

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
delegation take the matter up with the Foreign Relations
Committee of the Senate. Miss Fabyan replied that they
had already interviewed a number of Senators and had been
told that the Senate was now awaiting a reply to their
request for Secretary Hull's views on the Pittman Reso-
lution, thus intimating that Congressional action was
dependent upon action by the State Department. Mr. Sayre
replied that nevertheless, in his opinion, the responsi-
bility for decision in this matter rested with Congress.
To an inquiry as to whether we were not justified,
if the Resolution were enacted, to proceed to carry it
out without taking steps to divest ourselves of our
obligations under the commercial treaty of l9ll, Mr.
Sayre indicated that there was involved in the matter
inter alia the position which this Government had oon-
sistently taken as to respect for the sanctity of
treaties. After some further brief discussion the
delegation appeared satisfied that it was to Congress
that they must look for action on this question and in-
dicated that they would pursue the matter further with
the members of the Foreign Relations Committee.

July 21, 1939
The President
The White House
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. President:
I have been working to secure restriction of export
to Japan of materials essential to her invasion of China,
particularly petroleum products, metals, motor equipment
and metal working machinery.
I do not know what your views are on this matter. I
can understand that it might not be wise for you to say
just now, considering the partisan attitude of many in
894.24/69
Congress.
Retention of the embargo in the neutrality law les-
sens your power to help keep peace in Europe. Something
could be salvaged if you were given discretionary powers
to stop the export of war materials to Japan. Britain
would probably join if you were able to promise Amerioan
action. Joe Grew apparently does not trust the British to
come through if we aot first, but I believe he 1s wrong, in
5
view of the present situation in the Far East. Danger of
Japan making war on us in reprisal is negligible. See
what she has taken from Russia without going to war. War
with us now would ruin Japan without a shot being fired.
894.24
À successful embargo on all war materials to Japan
would in six months or a year weaken Japan seriously. If
Japan is weakened on Russia's rear, Germany will have to
be more cautious, and you might acoomplish part of what
you wanted.
F/A
Senator Pitman favors suoh a move and would have some
support that he lacked on the neutrality law, partioularly
Senators Gillette and Reynolds.
2
6
measures, and doubtless other Democrats with them, partly
because they do not know the attitude of the administration.
If you think the measure desirable, could you not in some

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
way make your supporters in the House understand this
and get them to back the bill without possibly embarrassing
publicity for yourself? I believe the matter is urgent in
view of the probable early adjournment of Congress.
If nothing is done and war 1s avoided in Europe be-
fore Congress reassembles, the opposition will feel
confirmed in their assertions that the danger was
exaggerated. If the Japanese embargo is passed, that
should serve as a check to such "I told you so" remarks.
Sincerely yours,
ROGER S. GREENE

My dear Mr. Welles:
Department of State
Am enclosing herewith a copy of an editorial appearing in the
Philadelphia Record. With this analysis of the situation in China I quite
agree. of course, I assume that it will be merely another contribution to
your waste basket. But at least it will afford me the same sort of satis-
faction in having registered my protest as Secretary Hull must receive in
protesting the ruthless action of the Japanese army in China with particular
reference to the lives and property of Americans.
At the same time it is a pleasure to note that the State Department
has acquiesced in the recent action of the Senate in abrogating the commer-
cial treaty with Japan. It is my understanding that the terms of this
treaty will act as a bar to our i tholding shipment of such raw materials
as cotton, copper, scrap iron etc. until after January of 1940. However,
there is no such bar to placing an ambargo upon the shipment of munitions,
except the ficticious notion that a state of war does not exist in China.
No realistic appraisal of the situation in China could fail to so define it.
89
To persistently ignore that fact and to fail to enforce the plain terms of
the Neutrality Act accordingly constitutes a flagrant violation of every
4.24/697
accepted principle of logic and ethics and every dictate of common sense.
But no doubt your office has been receiving like criticism of the foreign
policy of the administration ad infinitum to the effect of which Mr. Hull
has become immune. None the less, if the flood of protests from canaid
observers of current events do not impress either the President nor the
Secretary, one would suppose that the logic of those events would do so.
Very truly yours,
AUG141939

DATA RELATING TO THE SUGGESTED EMBARGO UPON
THE EXPORTATION OF WAR MATERIALS TO JAPAN
The amended joint resolution introduced by
Senator Pittman on July 10, 1939, would empower the
President to restrict or prohibit exports of the follow-
89
ing articles and materials: arms, ammunition, and
munitions of war, iron, steel, oil, gasoline, scrap
6
1ron, scrap steel, and scrap metal. It is suggested
at the outset that logical additions to the list are
copper and automobiles; these items are of prime
importance to Japan's military operations, and the United
894.24/69
States 1s by far the principal supplier of Japan's
imports.
There follows a table showing exports from the
United States to Japan in 1937 and 1938 of the foregoing
materials and articles and the percentage of all countries'
8
exporte to Japan in those years supplied by the United
States and other 1mportant suppliers. These statistios
are taken from a report prepared recently by a staff
under the direction of Dr. Hu Tun-yuan, a Harvard-trained
statistical economist connected with Brookings Institution
FILED

71939
accurately

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
accurately done. The statistios under discussion were
compiled from official statistios of foreign countries
and converted into United States dollars.

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
The foregoing statistics, of course, relate only to
the importetion into Japan of the articles and materials
in question and constitute only one factor in the question
of Japan's supply of these commodities. The question of
supply involves production within the Japanese Empire
and 1n territories in China controlled by Japan. A
collateral question of substantial importance is that of
alternate sources of supply (other than the United States).
On the basis of a more extended study of the question
of Japan's supply of essential war materials (FE's
memorandum of date April 20, 1939, entitled "The Matter
of an Import and Export Embargo against Japan"), the
following general estimates are offered in regard to the
1mportance of imports from the United States to Japan's
total supply of certain commodities involved in the
joint resolution.
Iron and Steel
It 1s estimated on the basis of available statistics
that two-thirds of Japan's iron ore requirements, one-
fourth of her pig iron, and one-half of her sorap iron
must be imported from outside the yen bloo. However, 1t
1s estimated that the United States supplies practically
none of Japan's imports of iron ore and about halr her
1mports of pig 1ron and sorap iron. The United States,
then,

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
then, furnishes none of Japan's supply of iron ore, about
one-fourth of her supply of scrap iron, and one-eighth of
her supply of pig iron. Alternate sources of supply
seem to be inadequate. There are other substantial pro-
ducers of 1ron ore in the Pacific area but Japan already
takes a substantial portion of the production of these
countries and competition is sharp for the remaining
production. Similarly, a brisk competition exists for
European production and Japan could probably obtain sub-
stantial additional supplies from Europe only at great
cost, if at all. However, it is hardly probable that an
embargo upon the exportation of 1ron ore would be of
1mmediate effectiveness in Japan as there are undoubtedly
substantial stocks available there and by sharp economy
the Japanese could probably hold out for a considerable
period. A shortage of iron would probably engender in
the Japanese economic and military machine a degree of
inefficiency which would tend to increase steadily.
Petroleum Products
At least two-thirds of Japan's supply of petroleum
products (probably more) must be imported from outside
the yen bloo and of total imports the United States
supplies at least sixty percent. It is estimated, there-
fore, that at the present time Japan 1s dependent to the
extent

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
extent of approximately forty percent upon American
supplies of petroleum products. It is believed that the
Japanese navy possesses at least three years' stook of
petroleum products and that there is in Japan a supply
of petroleum products for commercial purposes which would
last approximately six months. If the United States
should declare an embargo upon the exportation of
petroleum products to Japan 1t would probably be reflected
almost immediately in a certain amount of inefficiency
in the Japanese air force and economic organization;
naval officials are of the opinion that Japan would not
be willing to deplete her reserves. However, there are
extensive alternate sources of supply for petroleum
products, although there might be a certain amount of delay
in arranging for supplies from these sources.
Copper
In 1936 the United States supplied about 90 peroent
of Japan's imports of copper. Consumption of copper in
Japan has undoubtedly increased enormously since 1936, and
it 1s doubtful that Japan's relatively meager deposits
have yielded a production equivalent to the increased con-
sumption. It is possible that the United States now
supplies half Japan's consumption of copper, and an Amer-
1can export embargo on copper would probably be a serious
matter for Japan, although substantial supplies might be
obtained

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.97)
-7-
obtained from other sources, such as Northern Rhodesia,
Canada, Chile, Peru, Belgian Congo and the Soviet Union.
Automobiles and Parts
It appears that the United States supplies the
larger part of Japan's requirements of motor vehicles for
military purposes. In 1937 the United States supplied
over 90 percent of Japan's requirements for military pur-
poses. There 1s some local production of trucks, a sub-
stantial production of small-size cars and a large local
production of cycle cars, but the unanimous opinion of
American officials in China is that the military uses
almost exclusively foreign trucks and automobiles because
of the unreliability of the local product, and that the
life of a motor vehicle in army use is very short. In
1938 1t appears that the Japanese imported substantial
quantities (30 percent of the total) of automobiles from
Germany. An embargo upon automobiles by the United
States would probably considerably embarrass the Japanese
military operations in China and cause a growing
inefficienoy in those military operations, but would not
necessarily cause any breakdown in view of the local
production and the possibility of increased imports from
Germany, Italy, France and the United Kingdom.

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
BISHOP LUCEY ASSAILS 'ISOLATION', 'BLIND NEUTRALITY'
PROPOSALS AS CONTRARY TO CATHOLIC TEACHING
Amarillo Prelate, In Address Here, Insists America Cannot Ignore
'International Immorality'; Urges Policy of 'Keep
United States Out of War by Keeping
War Out of the World'
Assailing the theory that 'isolation' or 'blind neutrality' should form
the basis for the foreign policy of the United States, Most Rev. Robert E. Lucey,
Bishop of Amarillo, Tex., in a radio address delivered over Pittsburgh station KDKA
last evening, insisted that such a policy cannot be reconciled with Catholic teaching,
that it is "stupid" and "immoral," and that it is idle for the United States to think
that it can keep clear of "international crime" by ignoring it. Quoting the "Papal
Peace Program" set forth by Pope Benedict XV and Cardinal Gasparri in 1917, Bishop
Lucey proposed as the proper course for this country the policy "Keep America out of
war by keeping war out of the world."
Bishop Lucey is in Pittsburgh this week to conduct one of the courses of
the School of Social Action for Priests being held at Central Catholic High School,
which will continue through next week. Very Rev. M. A. McGarey, of Wilmerding,
chairman of the priests' committee appointed by Bishop Hugh C. Boyle to arrange for
the school, introduced Bishop Lucey to the radio audience.
The 'Papal Peace Program'
Bishop Lucey began by quoting Pope Benedict's statement of the right basis
for international relations, which the Pontiff included in the appeal he issued to
the belligerent nations on August 1, 1917:
"First of all, as a fundamental principle, moral right must be substituted
for the material force of arms. Out of this shall arise a just agreement for a
simultaneous and reciprocal diminution of armaments according to rules and guarantees
to be laid down hereafter, without impairing, however, the force needed for the
maintenance of public order in each state. In place of armed force should be substi-
tuted the noble and peaceful institution of arbitration according to regulations to
be made, and penalties to be imposed upon any state which might refuse either to sub-
mit a national question to such a tribunal or to accept its decision."
The international tribunal referred to should have, according to Cardinal
Gasparri, then Secretary of State, as quoted by the Bishop, effective power of
"boycotting morally, economically, industrially, and financially all countries mani-
festing a militaristic spirit." Bishop Lucey continued:
"As I read those words about applying sanctions to militaristic nations I
recall a certain Chicago speech in which the President of the United States declared
that we should "quarantine the aggressors." It is obvious that Pope Benedict XV,
Cardinal Gasparri, and the President of the United States have thought in terms of
international co-operation for peace.
"Today, in our great country there is much talk of isolation and neutrality.
Those citizens who preach isolation are without any doubt thoroughly sincere; they
are not aware that there is no such thing as isolation. Those citizens who preach
neutrality are earnest and honest; they do not realize that neutrality in the face
of crime is itself a crime.
一1-

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
"Why is there no such thing as isolation? Because every one of us has
been born into the family of nations and we can't got out of it. The brother or the
father in a family may desert the domestic circle, but they are brother and father
still. We cannot shake off our membership in the human race. Every man is our
brother regardless of where he lives, and we cannot escape the obligations of
brotherhood.
"But what about neutrality? We have no hesitation in declaring
that neutrality in the face of international crime is stupidity
and immorality combined.
"We as a people do see some difference between right and wrong. We as a
people beliove that unprovoked aggression cries to heaven for vengeance; that the
brutal use of force is an intermational crime and because it is a crime we condemn
it with all our heart and soul. Morally speaking we cannot be neutral as between
right and wrong, truth and falsehood, war and peace.
"The isolationist says: 'Let the rest of the world stew in its own juice.
What happens in other countries is no concern of ours. Let them start a war if they
will, but we must keep out of it.' The internationalist says: 'What happens in other
countries is surely of interest to us. War in any part of the world is very much
our concern, first, because the warring men are our brothers, and secondly because
we may be dragged into it.' In a word, the internationalist says: 'Keep America
out of war by keeping war out of the world.' That makes sense.
'International Poker Game'
"Today a tremendous game of international poker is being played in the
world. Certain aggressor nations, frightfully armed and ready, are using the threat
of force to achieve their ends. The so-called democratic powers are prepared to
arbitrate any claims or difficulties that may be proposed. If America, the most
powerful nation in the world, will let the aggressors know that our sympathies will
be with the peace loving nations in the event of war, and that we will sell munitions
to the victims of aggression, then there will be no war. The mere threat of our
munitions may be all that stands between Europe and chaos.
"As a follower of the Prince of Peace, I hate war and the imple-
ments of war. Your prayer and mine must be that Europe may be
saved from the horrors and devastation of another armed conflict.
If the mere threat of selling munitions to the peaceful nations
may avert a war then in the name of peace and civilization let
us utter the threat.
"Certain objections are raised against any action whatever in the field
world. The answer of course is evident. We have no intention that America should
police the world. We do demand, however, that unjust aggression be recognized; that
our country stop trading with international murderers; that we confine our trade in
time of war to the nations that stand for peace. That is not policing the world;
it is showing some self-respect.
Aggressors
"But the objector continues, How can you recognize unjust aggression?
You might make a mistake of judgment.' Again the answer is evident. If you can't
find the aggressor you can't punish him. But only too frequently the aggressor is
very much in evidence. Do you doubt who was the aggressor in Ethiopia? If hard
-2-

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
pressed could you find the aggressor in the tragedy of China? Were Bohemia and
Moravia taken by brute force? Who invaded and seized Albania? My friends, let us
not deceive ourselves. Even the man in the street could put his finger on the
aggressor in those wanton acts of cruelty.
"But our objector isn't satisfied. What right have we, he asks in righteous
indignation, to appoint ourselves judges of international morality?
"Does the objector mean to suggest that the average man does not know the
difference between right and wrong? The gentle Master who told us not to judge any
man also declared: "He that is not with me is against me." We must take our stand
for justice and right. If we do not support the world moral order the sense of
morality will perish. We do not set ourselves up as the judges of any man. It was
God Himself who set the standard: Thou shalt not kill.
"Our objector isn't finished yet. He now finds refuge in some good old
American slogans. We should mind our own business and stay in our own back yard.'
'We don't want any entangling alliances; they will drag us into War.'
'Minding Our Own Business'
"I quite agree that we should mind our own business; and it is very much
our business that we should make some effort in support of peace. We are the world's
greatest nation. We are still a democracy. We have power and prestige. We belong
to the family of nations, and our very position of leadership places upon us the
solemn obligation to try to keep the peace.
"As far as entangling alliances are conceined there is no necessity to
entangle ourselves in anything, but it would be well for us and for the world if we
supported peace loving nations in their efforts to restrain militaristic governments.
We need not and shall not go to war, but we ought to make it abundantly clear that
America loves liberty; that America knows right from wrong; and that America will
give neither support nor encouragement to governments that keep the world in terror."
Taken from "THE PITTSBURGH CATHOLIC"
Official Organ of the Diocese of
Pittsburgh -- Issue of Thursday,
July 20, 1939

"DOES JAPAN UNDERSTAND NOW?"
Editerial from the August 2, 1939 Issue of ZIoNS
HERALD -- published by the Boston Wesleyan Associe-
tion, 38l Boylston Street, Boston, Massachusetts.
Following Great Britain's recent pathetic surrender
to Japanese arrogance, the United States Government
suddenly gave notice on Wednesday of last week that
she was denouneing her treaty of 19ll wi th Japan.
Six months must elapse be fore the denunoiation be-
comes effective. By the terms of the treaty, trade
and navigation between the two ountries have been
regulated for the last twenty-eight years. The
United States has endured too long the seesaw of
studied insults to American citizens by the Japanese
in China and their deceitful apologies after the fact.
The honorable Japanese may possibly comprehend the
language used last Wednesday, however, dull though
they have been heretofore in underatanding appeals
to justice and fair play. An implication of the
treaty denunciation is that soon we may expe ot the
too-long-delayed embargo on war-making materials now
being shipped to Japan for the murder of China's
mil11ons. In 1937 = '38, $326,000,000 worth of sorap
iron, machinery, and other war-making materials went
to Japan from the United Sta tes. Blood money!
Boston -- yes, oultured Boston, which boasta of its
Puritan morality -- 1s annually shipping $26,000,000
worth of sorap iron to Japan. THE BOSTON HERALD on
Sunday presented its readers wi th a large pioture of
a gigantic heap of scrap iron in the Southampton Street
railroad years near the City Hospital whioh is about
to be shipped to an "overseas destimation." What firm
or firms 1n Boston are selling annually $26,000,000
worth of serap iron to Japan? Who are the officers
and directors of the company or companies? Are any
of them members of Pro testant or Catholio churohes or
Hebrew synagogues? The publio has a right to know.
Here is an opportunity for some olever news-hawk to
ferret out an interesting front-page story, and for
some enterprising newspaper to renter public service.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
PARTICIPATION IN AGGRESSION
Hundred American Bishops Deolare it Must Stop
New York: Two hundred Protestant, Catholic and Jewish religious
Teaders, including over one hundred Bishops, have signed a Declara-
tion opposing the continuance of American supplies to aggressor
nations. The statement was issued on July 12th by the Rt. Rev.
G. Ashton OLDHAM, Bishop of Albany, New York, and President of the
World Alliance for International Friendship through the Churches.
"Being concerned with the welfare of all peoples,"
the statement says, "we need to oppose direct or
indireot support of those governments which do en-
gage in war as an instrument of national polioy.
Aggression muat be opposed wherever it is threaten-
ed or manifested. To furnish the sinews of war to
aggressor states is a grave injustice, not only to
the immediate victims of aggression, but to all
peoples, be cause s ch aid encourages new aggressi ons
and the spread of war. Religious groups should, we
believe, urge upon their peoples and governments
that the price of peace includes the refusal to
profit from war by economic participation in ag-
gression."
Six Prinoiples
This policy is one of six principles urged by the re-
ligious leaders. The other five proposals advocate opposition
to war as an instrument of national poliey, humani tarian aid to
the viefims of war, opposi tion to racial or religious disorimina-
tions, resistance to injus tioe between or wi thin nations, and
support of adequate peace machinery.
In keeping with these prinoiples, the group endorses
the three-fold program of the American Union for Conoerted Peace
Efforts: to oppose aggression, to promote justice between nations,
and to develop adequate peace machinery.
Copies of the statement have been sent to the foreign
relations committees of the House and Senate.

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
BETHLEHEM STEEL CORPORATION
25 BROADWAY
R. E. McMATH
Vice President
NEW YORK, N. Y.
October 21, 1938
Dear Sir:
Mr. Schwab has asked me to reply to your
letter of October 13th.
We have shipped a very substantial tonnage
of steel products to Japan over the last few years
both in 1937 and 1938. Most of these products were
standard commercial steel products and I do not see
in what way our selling them has contributed importantly
to the development of that nation. Certainly, if we had
not sold them there are plenty of other companies in
this country and elsewhere that would have been glad
to do so. In any event, I believe that any action
taken toward limiting the export from this country to
Japan should be taken by the Nationa! Government and
not made a matter for action by individual companies.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) R. E. McMATH
Vice President.
Mr. Frederick C. McKee,
2215 Oliver Building,
Pittsburgh, Pa.

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
BISHOP LUCEY ASSAILS 'ISOLATION', 'BLIND NEUTRALITY'
PROPOSALS AS CONTRARY TO CATHOLIC TEACHING
Amarillo Prelate, In Address Here, Insists America Cannot Ignore
'International Immorality'; Urges Policy of 'Keep
United States Out of War by Keeping
War Out of the World'
Assailing the theory that 'isolation' or 'blind neutrality' should form
the basis for the foreign policy of the United States, Most Rev. Robert E. Lucey,
Bishop of Amarillo, Tex., in a radio address delivered over Pittsburgh station KDKA
last evening, insisted that such a policy cannot be reconciled with Catholic teaching,
that it is "stupid" and "immoral," and that it is idle for the United States to think
that it can keep clear of "international crime" by ignoring it. Quoting the "Papal
Peace Program" set forth by Pope Benedict XV and Cardinal Gasparri in 1917, Bishop
Lucey proposed as the proper course for this country the policy "Keep America out of
war by keeping war out of the world."
Bishop Lucey is in Pittsburgh this week to conduct one of the courses of
the School of Social Action for Priests being held at Central Catholic High School,
which will continue through next week. Very Rev. M. A. McGarey, of Wilmerding,
chairman of the priests' committee appointed by Bishop Hugh C. Boyle to arrange for
the school, introduced Bishop Lucey to the radio audience.
The 'Papal Peace Program'
Bishop Lucey began by quoting Pope Benedict's statement of the right basis
for international relations, which the Pontiff included in the appeal he issued to
the belligerent nations on August 1, 1917:
"First of all, as a fundamental principle, moral right must be substituted
for the material force of arms. Out of this shall arise a just agreement for a
simultaneous and reciprocal diminution of armaments according to rules and guarantees
to be laid down hereafter, without impairing, however, the force needed for the
maintenance of public order in each state. In place of armed force should be substi-
tuted the noble and peaceful institution of arbitration according to regulations to
be made. and penalties to be imposed upon any state which might refuse either to sub-
mit a national question to such a tribunal or to accept its decision."
The international tribunal referred to should have, according to Cardinal
Gasparri, then Secretary of State, as quoted by the Bishop, effective power of
"boycotting morally, economically, industrially, and financially all countries mani-
festing a militaristic spirit." Bishop Lucey continued:
"As I read those words about applying sanctions to militaristic nations I
recall a certain Chicago speech in which the President of the United States declared
that we should "quarantine the aggressors." It is obvious that Pope Benedict XV,
Cardinal Gasparri, and the President of the United States have thought in terms of
international co-operation for peace.
"Today, in our great country there is much talk of isolation and neutrality.
Those citizons who preach isolation are without any doubt thoroughly sincere; they
are not aware that there is no such thing as isolation. Those citizens who preach
neutrality are earnest and honest; they do not realize that neutrality in the face
of crime is itself a crime.
一1-

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.98)
"Why is there no such thing as isolation? Because every one of us has
in born into the family of nations and we can't get out of it. The brother or the
rather in a family may desert the domestic circle, but they are brother and father
still. We cannot shake off our membership in the human race. Every man is our
brother regardless of where he lives, and we cannot escape the obligations of
brotherhood.
* * * *
"But what about neutrality? We have no hesitation in declaring
that neutrality in the face of international crime is stupidity
and immorality combined.
"We as a people do see some difference between right and wrong. We as n
people believe that unprovoked aggression cries to heaven for vengeance; that the
brutal use of force is an intermational crime and because it is a crime we condemn
it with all our heart and soul. Morally speaking we cannot be neutral as between
right and wrong, truth and falsehood, war and peace.
"The isolationist says: 'Let the rest of the world stew in its own juice.
What happens in other countries is no concern of ours. Let them start a war if they
will, but we must keep out of it.' The internationalist says: 'What happens in other
countries is surely of interest to us. War in any part of the world is very much
our concern, first, because the warring men are our brothers, and secondly because
we may be dragged into it.' In a word, the internationalist says: 'Keep America
out of war by keeping war out of the world.' That makes sense.
'International Poker Game'
"Today a tremendous game of international poker is being played in the
world. Certain aggressor nations, frightfully armed and ready, are using the threat
of force to achieve their ends. The so-called democratic powers are prepared to
arbitrate any claims or difficulties that may be proposed. If America, the most
powerful nation in the world, will let the aggressors know that our sympathies will
be with the peace loving nations in the event of war, and that we will sell munitions
to the victims of aggression, then there will be no war. The mere threat of our
munitions may be all that stands between Europe and chaos.
"As a follower of the Prince of Peace, I hate war and the imple-
ments of war. Your prayer and mine must be that Europe may be
saved from the horrors and devastation of another armed conflict.
If the mere threat of selling munitions to the peaceful nations
may avert a war then in the name of peace and civilization let
us utter the threat.
"Certain objections are raised against any action whatever in the field
of international relations. We are asked, for example, why America should police the
world. The answer of course is evident. We have no intention that America should
police the world. We do demand, however, that unjust aggression be recognized; that
our country stop trading with international murderers; that we confine our trade in
time of war to the nations that stand for peace. That is not policing the world;
it is showing some self-respect.
Aggressors
"But the objector continues, How can you recognize unjust aggression?
You might make a mistake of judgment.' Again the answer is evident. If you can't
find the aggressor you can't punish him. But only too frequently the aggressor is
very much in evidence. Do you doubt who was the aggressor in Ethiopia? If hard
-2-

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
pressed could you find the aggressor in the tragedy of China? Were Bohemia and
Moravia taken by brute force? Who invaded and seized Albania? My friends, let us
not deceive ourselves. Even the man in the street could put his finger on the
aggressor in those wanton acts of cruelty.
"But our objector isn't satisfied. What right have we, he asks in righteous
indignation, to appoint ourselves judges of international morality?
"Does the objector mean to suggest that the average man does not know the
difference between right and wrong? The gentle Master who told us not to judge any
man also declared: "He that is not with me is against me." We must take our stand
for justice and right. If we do not support the world moral order the sense of
morality will perish. We do not set ourselves up as the judges of any man. It was
God Himself who set the standard: Thou shalt not kill.
"Our objector isn't finished yet. He now finds refuge in some good old
American slogans. We should mind our own business and stay in our own back yard.'
'We don't want any entangling alliances; they will drag us into War.'
'Minding Our Cm Business'
"I quite agree that we should mind our own business; and it is very much
our business that we should make some eficrt in support of peace. We are the world's
greatest nation. We are still a democracy. Wo have power and prestige. We belong
to the family of nations, and our very position of leadership places upon us the
solemm obligation to try to keep the peace.
"As far as entangling alliances are conceined there is no necessity to
entangle ourselves in anything, but it would be well for us and for the world if we
supported peace loving nations in their efforts to restrain militaristic governments.
We need not and shall not go to war, but we ought to make it abundantly clear that
America loves liberty; that America knows right from wrong; and that America will
give neither support nor encouragement to governments that keep the world in terror."
Taken from "THE PITTSBURGH CATHOLIC"
Official Organ of the Diocese of
Pittsburgh -- Issue of Thursday,
July 20, 1939

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
BETHLEHEM STEEL CORPORATION
25 BROADWAY
R. E. McMATH
Vice President
NEW YORK, N. Y.
October 21, 1938
Dear Sir:
Mr. Schwab has asked me to reply to your
letter of October 13th.
We have shipped a very substantial tonnage
of steel produots to Japan over the last few years
both in 1937 and 1938. Most of these products were
standard commercial steel products and I do not see
in what way our selling them has contributed importantly
to the development of that nation. Certainly, if we had
not sold them there are plenty of other companies in
this country and elsewhere that would have been glad
to do so. In any event, I believe that any action
taken toward limiting the export from this country to
Japan should be taken by the National Government and
not made a matter for action by individual companies.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) R. E. McMATH
Vice President.
Mr. Frederick C. McKee,
2215 Oliver Building,
Pittsburgh, Pa.
