Third-Term Song
The third-term boom has been
put to music. Jay Gorney, Holly-
wood song-writer who turned out
the hit, "Brother, Can You Spare
a Dime?" has written its theme
song, "Mr. Roosevelt, Won't You
Please Run Again." The words are
by Henry Meyers.
The campaign to popularise it is
being pressed with vigor. Over
2000 records were sold nt the
Young Democrats' Convention in
Pittsburgh, and big band presenta-
tions have been arranged at both
San Francisco and New York fairs.
—DREW PEARSON and
ROBERT A. ALLEN.

=== Page 185 === (confidence 1.00)
WE CAN'T WIN, BUT WE MIGHT
LOSE
Latest news from China and Japan makes it
clearer than ever that we should get out of the
Far East—withdraw bag and baggage, gunboats
and salesmen, before war mongers have a chance
to revive the cry of "Remember the Maine" over
the sinking of some 1000-ton gunboat in an ob-
scure Chinese river.
The Japanese, bogged down in their war against
China, are arousing sentiment among the Chinese
against all foreigners. On Sunday there was a
demonstration against Americ ins in the Japa-
nese-controlled territory in the province of
Shansi. This Thursday a meeting of Chinese
business men will be held in the Japanese-con-
trolled seaport of Tsingtao to consider a resolu-
tion denouncing American abrogation of the 1911
treaty with Japan.
For months the Japanese were comparatively
careful of the feelings of Americans (since the
Panay incident). While Britons were being
humiliated in Tientsin, Americans were being
treated with consideration.
But with abrogation of the treaty, Japanese
are out in the open with anti-American propa-
ganda. How long will it be before Japanese
planes bomb more American ships, as they did
two British ships in the Yangtze River on Sunday?
Suppose Japan wins the war with China. The
Japanese, recalling American aid to China in the
form of loans and supplies, won't welcome Aner-
ican business men, and there will be an end of
the little business we have with China. Abroga-
tion of the 1911 treaty already masks the end of
normal trade with Japan.
Suppose China wins the war. If she emerged
with her new national unity stronger than ever,
how long would she allow Americans or any other
foreigners their special rights in treaty ports and
their special trade concessions? When that time
came, would we join hands with Japan nd Ger-
many and other foreign nations to put down an-
other Boxer Insurrection?
We can't win either way, but we might easily
become involved in a war not of our making.
Our total exports to Japan the first six months
of this year totaled only $111,036,000. Our two
super-battleships now being built will cost nearly
$180,000,000.
Considered merely as a matter of economics,
without thinking of American sailors, what is
there in the Far East worth our fighting for?
It is nearly two weeks since the 1911 treaty was
renounced. In that time nothing has been done to
remove American gunboats, American business
and American citizens from Japan and Japanese-
controlled territory in China.
But in the meantime we have gone on supplying
Japan with steel and oil and scrap iron.
What are we waiting for?
Are we going to gamble the peace and pros-
perity of this country in a futile attempt to keep
that trade with the Orient?
While we write notes to Japan, Germany is
making hay in South America—where we should
be concentrating our efforts.
Only last week Germany concluded another
trade treaty with a South American country,
this time with Bolivia. After completing the
Bolivian pact, the German emissary moved on to
Lima to discuss a trade pact with Peru.
The slogan "Asia for Asiatics" won't worry us,
so long as we answer with "America for Amer-
icans,—and that goes for both hemispheres."
And we don't need warships to trade with
South America.

Mr. Maxwell M. Hamilton, Chief,
Division of Far Eastern Affairs,
State Department, Washington, D. OMMJNICATIONS
AND RECORDS
My dear Mr. Hamilton:
Permit me to thank you for your letter of August 7th in re-
ply to mine of August 2, 1939. Enclosed herewith are three clippings
from the Philadelphia Record which afford ample evidence of the unjust-
ifiable character of the present and past policies of the administration
with regard to the actual "state of war" in both China and Spain.
In the explanatory statement accompanying your letter of Aug.
7th, the items specified for embargo include arms, ammunition, and im-
plements of war, tin-plate scrap or helium gas. To quote further: "I
determining not to make such a finding (of a state of war) ----the Pro-
ident has exercised the discretion vested in him by the Act" In making
such a decision the President must have simply closed his eyes to the
most obvious of facts which were common knowledge to everyone who read
the regular press reports. These facts must also have been known to
the state department through consuler reports. In my view as well as
that of all who support a neutrality policy, both the President and
Secretary Hull have grossly abused the power given them by the Act in
question. And had they not bitterly opposed the last Congress in the
matter of revising the Act, the power to embargo all the items above
mentioned as well as cotton, scrap iron, copper etc. would have been
written into the law. No apoligies or explanations can condone for this
wilful evasion of the plain intent of the Act. And in the end the whel
country will suffer irreparable loss as a result.
Also the more recent loan made to General Franco, through the
Export-Import Bank,of $13,750.000.00 to bolster his shaky regime 1
In the language of the street, this loan was simply a "holaup end m
any discerning person would anticipate that the whole f the i
would ultimately find its way into Germany in payment of Franco's debt
to the Germans for help in subjecting Spain.
Very truly yours,
.EHyslop.

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
NOTES ON THE NATTONAL
DEFENSE
With approval by Congress, the Fegeral Gov-
ernment is engaged in a $1,800,000,000 program
for improving our national defense.
We're for that program. So, we belleve, are
most people.
Last week the War Department announced
completion of plans to begin the new $300,000,000
program of army airplane building.
A recent dispatch from Santa Monica, Calif.,
stated:
"America's largest land plane, the Doug-
las C4. has been sold to Japan. Reports are
that the deal included plans from which
Japan will be able to manufacture her own
planes of this type."
Another dispatch from Washington:
"Naval supply experts said today that large
exports of petroleum to foreign countries
have seriously depleted the available fuel
supply on the Pacific Coast.
"Much of the depletion comes from exports
to the Orient, especially to Japan."
From Rome we learn that the Italian Govern-
ment is financing a $600.000,000 purchase of scrap
iron and steel in the United States.
Three weeks ago the Federal Government,
through the Export-Import Bank, advanced ap-
proximately $14,000,000 to the Spanish Govern-
ment to buy U. S. cotton. The Spanish Govern-
ment is an ally of the Rome-Berlin axis.
During the past year, according to official
figures, Japan bought 57 percent of all her war
supplies in this country.
A few days ago the U. S. Army staged important
war games down around the national capital
and at Plattsburg.
We wonder whether those games are, in their
way, grimly prophetic of what we may expect
in the next war?
Both sides armed with American munitions!
Welcome—and Remember
When the Marquess of Lothian, new British
Ambassador to the United States, reached New
York on Tuesday he asked ship news reporters
not to hold against him previously expressed pri-
vate opinions, now that he was in an official
position.
We are perfectly willing to forget-at least
until we are forced to remember—that Lord Lo-
thian was formerly considered a member of the
"Cliveden set"; that he was once a whole-hearted
believer in the appeasement policy that led from
Munich to the present crisis; that he has been
accused by Labor members of Parliament of being
pro-Nazi.
But there is one previously expressed opinion
that we are not going to allow the new Ambas-
sador to forget. That is a speech he made be-
fore the Pilgrims Society in the Hotel Victoria in
London July 13, when he was Ambassador-desig-
nate. After kindly advising his audlence "to con-
cede to the United States just the same freedom
and independence as we claim for ourselves," he
said of the crisis:
'It is essential that we should realize that the
United States is going to think out these ulti-
mate problems for itself, and by itself, and that
it is going to reach somewhat different conclu-
sions from ourselves."

right side of the skull led two Italian scientists
to believe that he was killed by a club during

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
Spanish Cotton
The State Department won't
admit it yet, but information has
leaked back from Madrid strongly
indicating that the quarter of a
million bales of cotton which the
Export-Import Bank is financing
for General Franco is being ship-
ped on to Germany.
Cotton is one of the most im.
portant raw materials needed by
Hitler in wartime, and several
members of the Roosevelt Cabi-
net—chiefly Morgenthau and
Ickes—vigorously opposed the
Spanish credit, pointing out that
Franco was merely a blind for
Hitler.
However, those in the State De-
partment who have fought secret-
ly for Franco since the start of
his career were able to put the
deal across—to the tune of $13.-
000,000.Interesting thing to
watch will be whether the State
Department acts to shut off the
undelivered portion of the cotton
shipments to Franco.
War Surprise
There was one big difference
between the outbreak of this war
and that of 1914—the State De-
partment was thoroughly aware
that it was coming. Many news-
papers, most of Congress, and
various other observers could not
believe that war was just aroun
the corner, but the State Depart-
ment reported to Roosevelt, as
early as midsummer, that war
looked almost certain.
Contrast this with the message
sent by American Ambassador
Gerard from Berlin on July 27,
1914:
"I have reason to believe mat-
ters will be arranged without a
general European war."
Next day, July 28, 1914, Austria
declared war on Serbia.

There are 5o0 tons of feldspar in Manzanillo at
the present time for shipment to Japan. The Japanese
have secured an option on 2oo0 tons a month of feld-
spar, and it is expected that this will be taken up
in case the quality is all right. It is understood
that feldspar is used in the manufacture of s teel,
pottery manufacture, and for war gases.
The CROM stevedores in Manzanillo have been ap-
proached regarding handling charges on scrap iron.
The deal is to cover between 80 and l00 thousand tons
of railway scrap iron, shipments to be over a period
of one year. The contract is to call for shipments
of 10,O0o tons a month. The National Railways are
the only ones that it is felt are in a position to
handle such an order.
The contract is being discussed by a large Japan-
ese firm in Japan, but it is understood that it is for
the Japanese Government. The Mexican Government has
made a number of concessions, one of them being the
withdrawal of export duties.
The New York representative of the Japanese firm
is expected to arrive in Mexico City within a short
time.
RALEIGH A. GIBSON

SEND GOLD RESERVE IMMEDIATELY
It is still fresh in our memory that when the

P.S. - Since this letter was dictated, the Russo-Japanese
border truce has been announced. While it is too early to
appraise the full significance of this development, it
seems clear that Japan, relieved to some extent of
immediate pressure upon the Manchuria border, may be en-
abled to increase military pressure against China. In
consequence, the threat to China's integrity and the sig-
nificance of America's role as Japan's armorer are both
substantially increased. Will you please take this as a

=== Page 206 === (confidence 1.00)
WAR IN EUROPE -- WHAT ABOUT ASIA?
The rapid march of events makes necessary a careful reappraisal of
the outlook in the Far East. What follows is not a comprehensive analysis.
It is an attempt to suggest some highlights of the changed situation. Com-
ment upon it will be appreciated.
Japanese Isolation
Conclusion of the non-aggression pact between Germany and Russia has
weakened, if not broken, all bonds between Japan and Germany. Japan is left
without an important diplomatic or military ally, save possibly Italy which
is not strong in the Far East. Japan is now also much more exposed to Russian
power on the North.
The outbreak of war in Europe further isolates Japan from the bene-
fits of European supplies and markets, increasing her dependence upon the
United States.
The effects of these changes upon Japanese policy are not yet fully
apparent. Abandonment of the anti-Comintern alignment is reflected in the
change of Cabinet, and the declaration of Japanese neutrality in Europe. Two
new powerful incentives toward some conclusion of the war in China now exist:
desire to be prepared against a strategically stronger Russia, and desire to
recoup economic losses through profits on European war-time trade. With the
realization that an American embargo upon war trade with Japan would serious-
ly cripple the China campaign, an active conciliatory policy toward the United
States appears to be taking shape. Blandishments will follow. There is no
reason to believe that these shifts in policy are more than opportunistic or
to suppose that there is any fundamental change in the attempt to establish
a "New Order" in Asia with ultimate exclusion of Western (including American)
interests and influences.
Russian Power
Russia's present neutrality in Europe and her presumed safety against
immediate involvement in war against Germany greatly increases her potential
power in the Far East. With Japan gradually approaching economic exhaustion
from the war against China, Soviet Russia is free at any time to extend her
long-standing feud with Japan into a major war. Whether she will wish to do
so is problematic, but the possibility is an extremely significant factor in
the total situation.
British and French Preoccupation in Europe
The Japanese Navy is quick to recognize, of course, the increased ex-
posure of British and French interests in China as well as throughout their
entire Asiatic empires. Removal of the tiny British and French defense forces
in China has already been demanded. There can be little doubt that if and when
opportunity permits (perhaps later in the European war) there will be further
pressure.

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
-2- War in Europe -- What About Asia?
Fear has been expressed lest Britain make a "deal" with Japan involving ces-
sation of aid to China, recognition of Japanese special influence there, and even
positive aid to Japan in return for guarantees against attack upon Hongkong, Malaysia
(including Singapore), the East Indies and Australasia. Britain's position in the
Far East, however, may not be as weak as at first appears. Whether she is forced to
yield ultimately to Japanese pressure probably depends upon three main factors. If
Italy remains neutral in Europe, the German blockade may not require the full British
and French fleets, in which case units of these fleets may be available for defense
purposes in the Far East. So long as Russia remains neutral, the Japanese may hesi-
tate to undertake new commitments which would further lessen their potential strength
against Russia. The degree of continued firmness in American policy will remain nec-
essarily a major factor in Japanese calculations, especially in connection with any
moves which might further threaten American interests or alienate American sympath-
ies.
American Influence
Probably no effect of the European war is more significant for the Far East
than the increased importance which it gives to American influence in the Pacific.
Until now the dilemma of American Far Eastern policy continues. While public senti-
ment and diplomatic pronouncements are in thorough opposition to Japan's unprovoked
attack upon China, our economic influence (which really counts in war) is very pre-
ponderantly on the side of Japan. It is now quite generally known that in l937 Japan
secured 54%, and in 1938 56% of her imports of essential war supplies from the United
States, and that we afforded her greatest external market through which these pur-
chases were financed.
The relative importance of the United States as Japan's chief armorer is
greatly increased as a result of the European war. The announced transfer of a
$10,O00,O00. machinery order from Germany to the United States is only one indica-
tion of an inevitable trend. For the obtaining of sinews of war which she cannot
possibly produce in adequate quantity herself, Japan can no longer look to the British
Empire, France or Germany. To a much greater degree even than before, it will be de-
pendent upon the United States. The same is true with regard to markets for an im-
portant part of her export trade, upon which depends the financing of war purchases.
The excuse that if the United States embargoes war materials to Japan, orders
will be transferred elsewhere does not now have even the degree of validity which it
had before the outbreak of war in Europe.
More than ever, therefore, the United States holds the key to the Far Eastern
situation and responsibility for its part in that situation. It is an irony of cir-
cumstance that even with Germany no longer an aid to Japan, America should become her
sole important economic ally. Continued American support through the furnishing of
war materials and the financing of these purchases may lead to an indefinite pro-
longing of the war against China and to an ultimate degree of success for Japanese
militarism which would otherwise be utterly impossible. The firm withholding of such
support through the proposed embargo on war materials to Jepan and perhaps through
restrictions upon imports from Japan would lead even more quickly now to the bogging
down of Japan's military machine and to the emergence of an independent China as a
guarantee for our own security as well as for future peace in Asia. This outcome
would be further ensured through peaceful aid to China for relief and economic recon-
struction.
Sept. 14, 1939
America Committee for Non-Participation in Japanese Aggression
8 West 4Oth St, New York, N.Y.

Article I of the so-called Nine Power Pact of Feb-
ruary 6, 1922 recites that:
"The Contracting Powere, other than China, agree:
Art. I of
Nine
"(1) To respect the sovereignty, the independence,
Power Pact
and the territorial and administrative integrity of

or citizens of friendly States, and from counte-

States." (4 Treaties, etc. (Trenwith, 1938) 4872,

The treaty contains no provision for its enforcement
save that in article VII it is provided:
"The Contracting Powers agree that, whenever
a situation arises which 1n the opinion of any one
Art. VII
of them involves the application of the stipulations
of the present Treaty, and renders desirable discus-

frank communication between the Contracting Powers
concerned." (Ib1d. 4875.)
I. The treaty.
At the first meeting of the Committee on Pacific and
Far Eastern Questions of the Conference on the Limitation
of Armament, 192l-22, which adopted the Nine Power Pact,
the Chinese Government proposed certain "general principles
Suggestion
by China
to be applied in the determination of the questions relat-
ing to China", among which was the following: "The Powers
engage to respect and observe the territorial integrity and
political and administrative independence of the Chinese
Republic". (Conference on the Limitation of Armament (1922)
866.) At the second meeting of the Committee (November 19,
1921), Mr. Root stated that he had l1stened to the state-
ments of the various delegates with respect to the policies
of their respective governments toward China and that he
Root
"had been considering how to arrive at an expression of the
Rosolution
points covered". It appeared, he said, that "all were
agreed

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
agreed to respect the independence and territorial and
administrative integrity of the Chinese Republic", that
all were agreed to follow a policy tending to seoure to
China the fullest possible opportunity to develop an
effective form of Government; and that all were agreed
that there should be an equality of opportunity for the
commerce and industry of all nations in China. In the
past, he said, these same things "had been repeated over
that
and over again" and he thought/"it would be useful to
restate these principles all together". If the committee
could agree, he said, he would try to formulate them in a
single "Resolution". The "agreement to be framed", he
added, should be an "expression of the common purpose."
(Ibid. 880-882.) Mr. Koo, the Chinese delegate on the
Committee, stated that it was his understanding that in

It is, of course, possible to argue that the literal

wherein it stated that the Powers agree "to respect

Some might argue that for the United States to refrain from

principles set forth in article I of the treaty.
In my opinion the phrase "to respect" - or the phrase
"to observe" which Mr. Root explained was encompassed within
the meaning of "to respect" - does not necessarily mean that
the United States bound herself not to ship any article to
any country that might ultimately use such article in an
aggression against China, or to refrain from doing anything
that might be construed as adversely affecting the terri-
torial or administrative integrity of China. To me, such
a construction is far-fetched. Apparently, those who drafted,
adopted and approved article I of the treaty had no much
thought in mind.
It is frequently stated that under international law
Inter-
national
a state is obligated to respect the sovereignty, independence
law
and territory of other states.
Thus, in the last edition of Oppenheim's treatise on
International Law, it is stated:
". . . In

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
". . . In entering into the Family of Nations
a State comes as an equal to equals; it demands
that certain consideration be paid to its dignity,
the retention of its independence, of its terri-
torial and its personal supremacy. Recognition of
a State as a member of the Family of Nations in-
volves recognition of such State's equality, dignity,
independence, and territorial and personal supremacy.
. . All these qualities constitute as a body the
international personality of a State, and inter-
national personality may therefore be said to be
the fact, involved in the very membership of the
Family of Nations, that equality, dignity, inde-
pendence, territorial and personal supremacy, and
the responsibility of every State are recognised
by every other State.
". . . Again, there are exceptions to the pro-
tection of the qualities which constitute the inter-
national personality of the State, and these exoep-
tions are likewise characteristic of the position of
the States within the Family of Nations. Thus, in
time of war belligerents have a right to violate one
another's personality in many ways." (Oppenheim's
International Law (5th ed. by Lauterpacht) 219, 220.)
Hershey summarizes the matter as follows:
"Until recently, authorities were generally
agreed that there exist certain essential or funda-
mental rights and duties of States which underly the
positive rules and customs of International Law.
These rights (to which are attached corresponding
duties) are usually described as primary, inherent,
absolute, fundamental, essential, permanent, etc.
They were formerly identified with natural rights
and formed part of the so-called law of nature.
Some publioists now regard them as moral rather than
legal principles, and a few even deny them altogether.
But these rights have, in fact, a broader and deeper
signifioance than the ordinary positive rules of the
Law of Nations of which they are in large measure
the ultimate basis or source, and have even greater
obligatory force. Though far from absolute and not
inherently unchangeable, they are in the nature of
controlling or fundamental principles based upon
conditions essential to State existence and inter-
national life in our time.
The

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
"The most important of these fundamental rights
of State 1s that of existence, which involves the
rights of self-preservation and defense. To this
right there is attached the corresponding duty of
respecting the existence of other States . . .
"The second essential right of States is that of
sovereignty, or autonomy and independence. This right
involves the corresponding duty of respecting the
autonomy or internai sovereignty and the independence
or external sovereignty of other States . .
"The third essential right of sovereign States
is that of equality before the law.
"From the rights of sovereignty and equality
flows a fourth fundamental right of States -- the
so-called right to respect. A failure to observe
the forms of respect due a sovereign State 1s usually
regarded as an affront to its dignity, and may entail
serious consequences. .
"The fifth essential right and duty of Statesis
that of mutual commerce or intercourse." (Hershey,
Essentials of International Public Law and Organiza-
t1on (1927) 230-251.)
The question arises, is not the United States, apart
from the Nine Power Pact, under an obligation under inter-
national law to respect the sovereignty, independence, and
the territorial and administrative integrity of China? The
question was in fact raised at the outset in the discussions
of the treaty in the Senate. Senator Walsh of Montana intro-
duced the discussion by quoting the first subparagraph of
artiole I of the treaty and saying --
"When another of these treaties was under con-
sideration I advanoed the idea that there is a mere
declaration of a rule of international law which
would obtain even though it were not thus expressed
1n

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
in the treaty. My esteemed friend, the Senator

upon that matter and felt it important enough to
dilate upon on several occasions, asserting that
no such principle of international law was recog-
nized, and sustained his contention by references
to history, showing that nations had repeatedly
violated the territorial integrity of others by
invading their territory.
"Of course, Mr. President, regrettable as it
may be, that has often occurred; but it has not
occurred by right at all, but by wrong; and every
nation which invades the territory of another sets
up some reason to justify before the world what
otherwise would be deemed by all the world to be a
wrong on its part. That is the way the matter is
treated by the writers on international law."
Subsoquently Senator Underwood, referring to Senator
Walsh's position, stated:
". . . generally international law has never
made any such principle effective, and therefore
I say in faot, not theory, it has not existed in

". . . I am not taking issue with him [Senator

theory; I am taking 1ssue with him as a matter of
fact."' (62 Cong. Rec., pt. 5, pp. 4761, 4763.)
If it be granted that in theory, at least, a state is
under an obligation under international law to respect the
territorial integrity of every other state, as Senators Walsh
and Underwood were agreed was true, and if the line of reason-

in order that they may not be used to undermine the terri-
torial

=== Page 229 === (confidence 1.00)
-17-
torial and administrative integrity of China, then it

an aggressor country.
Probably this should be the case. I know of no inter-
national lawyer of repute, however, that would make such a
pronouncement as being declaratory of international law.
It 1s not even arguable that such an obligation exists
under international law at the present time, however moral
such action might be.

II. Authority of the President to embargo
goods destined to Japan.
It 1s urged that the President has authority to embargo
goods destined to Japan under sections 238 and 245 of Title
22 of the United States Code. The argument is based upon
Question
presented
the premise that under subparagraph 1 of article I of the
Nine Power Pact the United States is "obligated" to re-
frain from violating the territorial and administrative
integrity of China through shipments of arms or war
materials to Japan fur use in connection with the hostil-
ities in which she is engaged in China. The alleged viola-
tion consists of the sale and exportation to Japan by
American citizens, with the at least tacit permission of
the Government of the United States, of war materials which
are subsequently used in violating the territorial or ad-
ministrative integrity of Japan. It is urged that such
sale and exportation to Japan constitutes a failure "to
respect" the territorial or administrative integrity of
China and that the United States is therefore violating
its treaty obligation.
Senator Schwellenbach stated in the Senate on August 2,
1939:
"The whole thesis of my remarks is that by
reason of the nine-power agreement we have an
obligation upon us to respect the territorial
and administrative integrity of China; and that
we

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
we are violating that obligation. I present this

The agreement is binding not only upon our Govern-
ment but upon our citizens; and when our Government
sits by and permits our citizens to vlolate the
treaty by making the profit they can make out of
sending to Japan materials which we know are to be
used by Japan for the purpose of destroying China's
territorial integrity, I say we are violating the
Nine Power Agreement.
"I conclude this branch of the discussion with
the statement that we do have a treaty obligation to
observe to respect the territorial integrity of China.
Our goods are now being used for the purpose of
destroying China'ε territorial integrity. Goods
are being shipped by our citizens who are subject

In the case of United States v. Baltimore and Ohio
Southwestern Railroad Company Justice McKenna, delivering
the opinion of the Supreme Court, said:
". . . But the general rule is that penal
statutes must be strictly construed. It is a
familiar rule and need not be illustrated. The
words of the statute, certainly when they have a
sensible meaning and a definite and unmistakable
signification, as the words of the statute under
review have, marks its extent. We do not mean to
say that ambiguity in words may not be resolved
by the olear purpose of the statute.
"If, however, there be no ambiguity, the words
of the statute are the measure of its meaning. If
there be ambiguity, the character of the statute
determines for a strict or liberal construction.
A criminal statute is strictly construed. Courts
are not inolined to make 'constructive crimes'."
(222 U.8. (1911) 8, 13-14.)
In

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)

treaty. Accordingly, it would be necessary to construe
the terms of the treaty strictly. The particular portion
of the treaty which it is urged is being violated is, as
stated above, subparagraph 1 of article I wherein the
Powers agree "To respect the sovereignty, the independence,
and the territorial and administrative integrity of China".
These words do not on their face inhibit the exporta-
striot

construction
country for possible use in China. The verb "respect" in
its ordinary connotation means to "show esteem", to "show
regard or consideration for", to "refrain from interfering
with". (The New Century Dictionary, p. 1536.) Mr. Root,
the draftsman of the article, explained that the word
"respect" included within its compass the word "observe".
(Ante). Moreover, the treaty states that "The Contract-

It might be argued that any obligation assumed was assumed
by the "Contracting Powers". Mr. Root, in view of his long
experience as Secretary of State and as a draftsman, was
fully aware of the distinotion.often made in the treaties
to which the United States is a party between the obligations
assumed by the State and those assumed by its nationals.

Article VI of Hague Convention XIII, concluded
October 18, 1907, for example, provides that "The supply,
in any manner, directly or indirectly, by a neutral Power
to a belligerent Power, of war-ships, ammunition, or war
material of any kind whatever, is forbidden." (2 Treaties,
etc. (Malloy, 1910) 2352, 2359.) The provision relates
solely to the obligations of "a neutral Power". Nationals
of a neutral Power may, of course, ship such articles to
belligerents, subject to their seizure and condemnation
by an opposing belligerent. Our commercial treaties dif-
in many respects
ferentiate/between the rights procured or the obligations
ssumed on behalf of citizens or subjects and those pro-
cured or assumed by the Contracting Parties. Thus, the
treaty of 19ll between the United States and Japan repeatedly
speaks of the rights of the "citizens or subjects of each
of the High Contracting Parties" as distinguished from the
rights and obligations of "Each of the High Contracting
Parties". (3 Treaties, etc. (Redmond, 1923) 2712.)
If it had been intended at the time of the adoption
and approval of the Nine Power Pact, to mean by the first
subparagraph of article I thereof, that the Contracting
Powers agreed, if necessary, in order to respect China's
territorial and administrative integrity, that neither the
respective Governments nor their citizens or subjeots should
export anything to an aggressor country that might be used
to

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
to undermine such territorial and administrative integrity,
i.e., to impose sanctions, boycotts, or embargoes, it
could and should have been so stated. No such possible
meaning was apparently raised either in the Conference
or in the discussion of the treaty in the Senate.
In view of the fact that the "Contracting Powers"
agreed to respect the territorial integrity, etc. of China,
the fact that the meaning of the word "respect" is not
entirely clear as it is used in the treaty, the fact that
the obligation which it is now urged is included within
the scope of the provision was not advanced or intimated
either at the Washington Conference or in the discussions
in the Senate, the fact that such an obligation when read
in connection with the provisions of the United States
Code would impose a penalty of forfeiture upon goods and
vessels about to be taken out of the United States contrary
to the supposed obligation, the fact that it would be dif-
ficult to show that specific arms or other materials
destined for Japan would ultimately be used to undermine
the territorial and administrative integrity of China, it
would seem to be extremely difficult to hold that persons
who ship arms to Japan are violating the law and subject-
ing their property to condemnation under the law as it
now stands.
Shoula

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
-25-
Should a situation arise in which "arms or munitions
of war, or other articles" were being or intended to be
exported, or about to be exported, from the United States
in violation of a provision of a treaty or of international
law, reasonably interpreted, sections 238 and 245 of Title
22 of the United States Code might properly be invoked.
Those sections, as pointed out ante, refer generally to
the exportation of such articles "in violation of law".
The phrase includes within its scope treaty provisions
and international law.
I have not discussed the possibility that the Presi-
dent, or the Secretary of State, might request exporters
voluntarily to refrain from shipping war materials, or
other materials, to Japan. In July 1938 such action was
taken with respect to airplanes and aeronautical equipment
shipped to armed forces engaged in bombing civilian popula-
tions. So far as I am informed the legality of such a
request has not been questioned. No legal reason 1s per-
ceived why the scope of the request might not be broadened
should the Executive care to do so, although in so doing
he would of course run some risk that Japan might not re-
gard it as a friendly act and take some action on that
basis.
Le

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.92)
19625

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
There are no statutes now in eyistence authorizing the
placing of restrictions on the export to any country of mate-
rials other than those technically defined as arms, ammunition,
and implements of war. Eefore the export to any country of
other manufactured products, or of raw materials, such as scrao
iron, could be prohibited, new legislation would be require.
The Neutrality Act of May 1, 1937 prohibits the export of
arms, ammunition, and implements of war to countries between
or among which the President has found a state of war to exist.
Any embargo imposed under this Act must apply equally to all
the belligerents. In determining not to make such a finding
in re ard to the si tuation in the Far East, the President has
exercised the discretion vested in him by the Act, the primary
purpose of which is to safeguard the interosts of this country
and those of its nationals. It is not possible under the Act
to impose an embargo on exports to Janan alone as has fro-
quently been suggested. Any embargo under the Neutrality Act
must necessarily prohibit the export of arms, ammunition, and
imploments of war not only to Japan but to China as well. It
may bo of interest to note in this connection that since
July 1, 1937 the Department of State has issued licenses for
the export of arms, ammunition, and implements of war to
China in the value of $15.531,778.68 and to Japan in the
valuc of $11,388,484.78.

GEORGE WAY WILLIAMS

Mi ch1gan
October 12, 1939
My dear Senator Brown:
In following the debate over the repeal of the Arme
Embargo Section of the Neutrality Act, I am convinced
that the Senate 1s trying very hard to solve this problem
in a statesmanlike manner. Hence this letter is not written
with any idea of offering advice, nor of having this letter
count pro or con in the prevailing discussion.
I am, however, seriously wondering if, in our national
preoccupation with this debate, we are not losing sight
of the situation in the Orient, which I believe is even
more vital to our national interest.
As I understand it, upon the outbreak of hostilities
in Europe, we have greatly increased our already large
shipments of war materials to Japan, and that the essentials
so shipped will not be affected in any way by the passage
of the legislation now pending before the Senate.
As I further understand it, Japan 1s vitally dependent
upon these shipments from the United States, to carry on her
undeclared war against China and, regardless of what we
think of her action, Japan is using these materials to
destroy China in spite of the Nine-Power Treaty to which
Japan and the United States are signatories. In other words,
Japan has broken her treaty, and, bluntly, we seem to be
aiding her to destroy the sovereignty of another nation,
whose sovereignty that same treaty binds us to respect.
If I have this wrong, I shall be glad to be correctly
informed, but it looks like a very curious position for
this nation to be in.
I would appreciate your advice as to whether any
legislation 1s pending before the Congress which has a
bearing upon this situation.
It is needless for me to add that I am very much in
favor
The Honorable Prentiss M. Brown,
The United States Senate,
Washington, D. C.

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
favor of any action which will keep this nation from
serving as an arsenal to a nation carrying on a war
of aggression againet another nation with which
historioally, and on the basis of our enlightened
self-interest, we are on friendly terms.
Yours very truly,
GEORGE W. WILLIAMS

My dear Senator:
1939
I greatly appreciate your letter of August 31 with
enclosures.
I am familiar with your SJR 67 and while in Washing-
ton recently I took occasion to commend it. If neutrality
legislation is hereafter proposed, there should be incor-
porated in it elther your resolution or Section 1 of tho
Schwellenbach SJR 143.
743.94/14897
You are no doubt familiar with the latter which is
based upon our own obligations under the Nine Power Treaty.
894.24/721
Under the Schwellenbach Resolution our restriction
of export would not be limited to those for "use in war"
since articles of any nature whatsoever taken into a country
against that country's sovereign will, is a violation of
its sovereignty. Therefore we would not be faced with the
embarrassment--impossibility in some instances--of proving
contemplated war use of the materials, although such con-
templated use would be the fact.
894.21
On the subjeot of scrap iron I recognize your knowledge
and respect your opinion. There may have been a recent
change in the scrap iron situation however. In the month
Just closed, Japan exported through this port alone 22,891
tons of scrap iron at a cost of approximately $l4.00 a ton,
which is considerably more than double 1ts ordinary commer
cial price. Our information here is that it is not avail
able elsewhere in the quantity Japan requires. Our informa-
tion further is that pig iron as a substitute for sorap irgn
would cost Japan at least double as much.
5
There are two other materials which, we understand,
A
Japan cannot get elsewhere, namely, Pennsylvania high-octane
gasoline (laok of which would necessitate reconstruction of
her motors) and molybdenite, of which 143 tons of a value
of approximately $800 per ton were exported through this
port during August.
These

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
These facts would seem to confirm information we get
from reliable sources in the Orient, that Japan's inability
to get these three materials from us would materially
embarrass, if not stop her war.
You inquire whether I think the Pres1dent has author-
ity to stop these supplies by Executive order. I do.
The premises for my opinion are:
1. Our permitted export of materials with knowledge
that they are to be used in an assault on China's sover-
eignty and independence, constitutes our own violation of
our Nine Power Treaty obligation to respect that sovereignty
and independence.
2. This treaty, binding upon the United States, binds
also its citizens and subjects who sell and export these
materials.
3. A treaty, by Constitutional definition, 1s a law.
4. Section 238, Title 22, United States Code pro-
vides that collectors of customs, United States marshals,
" and every other person duly authorized for the purpose
by the President, may seize and detain any articles or
munitions of war about to be exported in violation of law."
The great value of this traffic to this port through
wharfage charges, stevedoring, insurance, banking, etc. has
brought an immense "moneyed" pressure upon Senator Schwellen-
back to desist from his anti-Japan activities. I fear that
this pressure is having some effect. But I trust that the
Senator may make a mental caloulation of the local profits
of the day as compared to the immense permament value to
the whole United States of a free and independent China.
Very sincerely yours,
FRED. H. LYSONS

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.98)
age 2 - Sec. of State from W. R. Wheeler - October 24, 1939
understand the situation, public opinion is against the purchase of imports from
Japan, through which the acquisition of war commodities is being financed.
I believe the "gallup poll" which was reported some months ago as repre-
senting seventy-five percent of our people being opposed to the trade indicated
above is correct.
I also believe that public opinion supports the act of the State Depart-
ment in giving notice of the cancellation of the Treaty of Commerce with Japan,
and that public opinion was truly expressed by Ambassa tor Grew in his remarks
reported this week from Tokio. I have not bt en abie to test the last statement, however.
At the annual meeting of the Foreign Missions Conference of North America
held last June in Swarthmore, PennsyIvania - the conference including in its mem-
bership representatives of approximately a hundred missionary societies and
organizations of the United States and Canada - the conference, after quite a
stormy discussion, voted practically unanimously in favor of a secession of ship-
ments of war supplies and war commodities to Japan, to be achieved by individual
action and agreement rather than by government coercion. The membership of the
conference is representative and influential, and I believe their vote is typical
of informed public opinion in both the United States and Canada.
I believe the American people support the State Department in its recent
positions in regard to American rights in China as affected by recent Japanese
actions, and I wanted to send this report to you at this time. I should say, I
am a Secretary of the Board of Foreign Missions of the Presbyterian Church in the
U.S.A., the largest of the Foreign Mission Boards in this country, but I am
writing not as an official represent tive of the board, but as an individual
observer of public opinion as it has been expressed in relation to the great issues
in the Far East.
Respectfully yours,

My dear Mr. Priestley:
In reply to your letter of October 2l, regarding & survey of domestic
resources of iron and steel scrap being conducted by this Department, and
the shipment of various commodities to Japan:
The present survey of iron and steel scrap covers the Month of
September, 1939, and was inaugurated in order to obtain reliable data on
available stocks of tnis essenti&l raw material for the manufacture of
steel. You can readily appreciate that under the conditions prevailing
abroad it is necessary for the Government to keep close watch on supplies
of all important and strategic raw materials. The rogram of investigation
has been endorsed not only by the Army and Navy Munitions Board, but by
trade associations representing various branches of the steel industry.
Preliminary results should be available the Latter part of November.
I am rateful to you for calling my attention to the viewpoint of
the China Club of Seattle in regard to our present trade with Japan.
Your comments on this subject, however, pertain to the foreign policy
of the United States. I am, therefore, referring your Letter to the
Department of State for consideration.
Sincerely yours,
KBURL
Actlug Secretary of the Interior.

CHINA CLUB OF SEATTLE

Y
October 21st 1939
Honorable Harold L. Ickes,
Department of the Interior,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
I understand that you are undertaking a survey of scrap
iron resources in the United States.
Last month through the port of Seattle we shipped 23,000
tons of scrap iron to the Japanese. In addition to this we
shipped molybdenite concentrates originating at the Climax mine
in Colorado to the value of $918,578.00 which is six times the
value of average shipments to Japan during the past year.
Molybdenite is necessary in the manufacture of projectile steel.
America is a partner with Japan in the destruction of
China because without the raw materials which we are shipping
to Japan the war in China would be impossible.
Those of us who know something of the situation were
hoping that Senator Schwellenbach's bill prohibiting the ship-
ment of these materials to the Japanese would have received
the support of the Administration. It may be that your attention
has not been brought to the bill.
It does seem the height of folly that we should be build-
ing up vast fortifications in the Pacific against the Asiatic
power and at the same time we are building up the military might
of Japan by the shipment of these essential raw materials,
Sincerely yours
CHINA CLUB OF SEATTLE
(Sgd) W. B. Priestley
W. E. Priestley
Honorary Secretary

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
Y
THE GUILT OF AMERICA
By W. E. Priestley
Across the Pacific Ocean an undeclared war is being fought. The
Japanese in China have killed at least one and a half million people,
thirty-five millions of people have been driven from their homes, and
untold losses in property have been accomplished by the Japanese war
machine.
Prince Konoye, premier of Japan, has stated quite frankly that
Japan's object is to beat China to her knees and every day Japan is
pursuing this ruthless policy against an innocent people.
The objectives of the Japanese army are quite clear and apparent.
To destroy all industry in China; to conquer the Chinese people and re-
duce them to a state of slavery; to destroy all foreign business in
China. In short, to reduce China to a state of vassalage under a cold-
blooded military dictatorship.
Sympathy of the American people is almost universally expressed
towards China in this terrible struggle. Every day Japanese planes
are flying over China, killing men, women and children who have no part
in this war.
It must be remembered that Japan is poor in raw materials necessary
to carry on a major war. She must look to other coumtries to supply her
with the necessary weapons to carry on such a war. Where is Japen getting
her materials to rape China?
Sixty per cent of the raw and finished materials used by the Japanese
are shipped from the United States. When the Japanese bombed the United
States ship "Panay" she used bombs made from American materials, she used
planes using American motors, and American gasoline was used in driving
those planes.
Through the port of Seattle last month we shipped half a million
dollars worth of material to Japan and 475,000 dollars of this was ma-
terials to be used for war purposes.
We shipped more scrap iron to Japan in six months than we have
shipped out of this country in any previous six-year period. America
is the silent partner in the invasion of China. Every day we are assist-
ing the Japanese army to carry out their destructive policy.

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.98)
OPY
What will happen if the Japanese conquer China? With the man power
and raw resources of China at their disposal, Japan will be a power to
be feared even by the United States. If Japan becomes strong enough she
will challenge us in the Pacific Ocean. In other words we are supplying
Japan with the sinews of war to help her build up her military strength,
thus enabling her to be in a position to attack our own possessions.
It is quite within the bounds of possibility that the very materials
we are shipping to Japan may be the weapons which she will use against
us in a future war.
It is high time that the people of these United States expressed
themselves in no uncertain veice. Not only should we stop being a partner
in the killing of the Chinese but we should realize that we are endanger-
ing our own homes as a result of our short-sighted policy.
Every decent American should protest to our State Department and
Say "STOP SHIPPING WAR MATERIALS TO JAPAN?"
C

P
Y

Mr. Joseph C. Green, Chief
LAHSEA

Department of State
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Green:
894.24/726
As I wrote you on September 23, 1938, The American Committee for
Non-Participation in Japanese Aggression is an American organization initiated
and controlled by Americans, and supported entirely by contributions from
American citizens. From August 1, 1938, to September 30, 1939, according to our
latest auditor's report, we have received 3,867 contributions which accounts for
most of our income from contributions and the sale of 1iterature of $54,892.73.
In addition, there was an excess over income from our Speakers' Account amount-
ing to 1,966.21. I should be happy to send you a copy of this auditor's report
and to make available to you any further information desired.
both for our own integrity and in order to avoid any possible charge
of violating, in the slightest degree, the present law, we have endeavored to
be meticulously careful since the formation of this Committee. There are three
types of cases, two of which have just been raised, upon which I should very
much appreciate a ruling from you. Naturally, Chinese in the United States are
keenly interested in the work of our Committee and we have gladly acceded to any
requests for information concerning our work, sending them samples of literature.
It is out of this interchange that the first two of the following questions have
developed:
A
(1) We have received through the mail today a contribution
of $5.00 from a Mr. Lowe with which he enclosed two con-
094.24
tributions of $25.00 and $6.00 from a Mr. Shoong and a
Mr. Foong. This is the first gift that we have had of
this kind. Mr. Lowe is, I believe, an American citizen,
FILED
born in this country of Chinese parents. The other two
men may also be American citizens although I have not
yet inquired about this. It has been our practice, in
5
connection with anyquestion of contributions, to say
CO
6
that we were happy to receive any donations from American
citizens but that we were not in a position to ask for
or accept contributions from any who were still Chinese
citizens. In a case of this kind, are we legally bound
to inquire as to the citizenship of each of the donors,
or can we accept these small gifts with thanks?
181

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.99)
oph C. Green, October 30, 1939.
(2) Another case was brought to my attention a few days ago.
A Chinese who is visiting the U.S. desired as a "token of
appreciation" to make a small personal contribution to the
work of our Committee. I asked that he withhold any such
contribution until I could make certain whether in accept-
ing it, we would be violating the existing law. Will you
kindly inform me as to the rule by which we should govern
our own conduct in a case of this kind?
(3) A further type of problem is presented by anonymous con-
tributions. We have received some anonymous gifts sent,
in every case so far as we have been able to cetermine, by
 American citizens. The total represented by all such
gifts amounts, I think, to considerably less than 10% of
our total income. We do not want to be foolishly cautious
about this matter; at the same time, I cannot be absolutely
certain about the original source of every one of these
gifts. To date, I have assumed that so long as any con-
tribution was sent to us by an American citizen and we had
no direct means of making an absolute check on the source,
we would be within our rights in accepting it. Of course,
some of these anonymous contributors have written in them-
selves to say, "Won't you consider this an anonymous gift?"
This has been from American frienus whom we knew directly or
through correspondence. There have been a few, however,
that have been sent in with some such statement as this:
"A friend has asked me to send you this contribution which
he would be glad to have considered as an anonymous gift
to the fine work of your Committee". The question that
I have in mind is: Are we obligated, under the terms of
the present law, to inquire into the source of every
contribution that may come to us in this way?
Of course, we could make an iron ruling not to accept any donation
of the types indicated above and this we should be glad to do, making it retro-
active, if that is necessary. On the other hand, if we are not required by law
to do this, it seems a little more gracious for us to accept these contributions,
and of course they do help even though their aggregate representa a very small
proportion of our total income.
Enclosed for your referen e are samples of the financial appeals which
we have sent rather widely to selected lists of people interested in the Far East
or international affairs generally. Enclosed also for your reference is a leaflet
which states the fundamentals of our program, in which I might refer you par-
ticularly to the section covering the program of this Committee.
Very sincerely yours,
Hay B. Price

Executive Secretary
One chapter in the campaign to stop American partioipation
in the supply of war materials and equipment to Japan was success-
fully completed before the adjournment of Congress last summer. The
demand of the people for the ending of this traffic had been demon-
strated, both by appeals from citizens to their Representatives in
Congress, as admitted by Congressman Bruce Barton, and by the Gallup
polls. The Administration was sufficiently impressed by the popular
support of our campaign to feel safe in terminating our commercial
treaty with Japan, which had been represented as an obstacle to the
proposed embargo. We are grateful for your generous support which
made this progress possible.
Thus the stage is cleared for further aotion on or after
January 26, 1940, when the treaty expires. The intervening period
can be used to good advantage in three principal directions: first,
in the preparation of additional and up-to-date publicity materials;
second, in bringing influenoe to bear in the most effective way
possible upon the Senators and Representatives in their home distriots:
and third, in resuming the active informational and promotional cam-
paign through the distribution of literature, special mailings, news-
paper releases, radio programs, consultations with influential oiti.
zens and, at the appropriate moment, further letters and delegations
to Washington.
These aotivities require the maintenance of our oentral
offioe in New York, and for that we need immediate funds, not only
for overhead, but for the preparation of literature, for wide-spread
correspondenoe with friends of the movement, for reorganization of
our mailing lists and for maintaining contact with developments in
Washington. Total expenditures from August 1, 1938, to June 30, 1939,
amounted to $48,603.30 of whioh $22,190.67 represented printing,
mimeographing, postage and mailing expenses, stationery, telegraphio
services, publicity and advertising, and $16,438.97, salaries of the
necessary staff, which at times of special aotivity inoluded as many
as 18 persons. At present the regularly employed staff has been re-
duoed to 7. The total colleotions to date, from contributions and
sale of 11terature, amount to 56,709.12 and the balance has been
almost exhausted during the 3 months that have passed since the end
181

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of June when the audited statement from which the above figures were
taken was drawn up. Detailed accounts, examined quarterly by certified
public accountants, are on file in the office; contributors who wish
further information are invited to inquire there.
We are now in urgent need of additional funds with which
to carry on the campaign, with sound preparatory effort in the immediate
future, and then with increased vigor as the next regular session of
Congress approaches. Our position is not unlike the familiar scene
at a river bank in North China, when a well-loaded cart has with great
labor been successfully dragged across a difficult ford, and the carter
is faced with the necessity of a renewed effort to draw the cart up a
steep, muddy bank until it can gain the level road for its onward
journey.
Will you not put your shoulder to the wheel and help this
Committee and the heavily burdened regular staff in New York to surmount
this last obstacle by contributing to the budget for this work as
your ciroumstances may permit?
8ncerely yours,
chairman
P. S. This letter is written in Chicago, but is being mailed from our
New York office to which all replies should be sent.

The American Committee
For No-Patction in Jaanee Agression
8 WEST 40TH STREET, NEW YORK, N. Y.

Hatry B. Price
Evccutive Secretary
The war waging in Asia may be as significant for the fut-
ure of humanity as that which is developing in Europe. Many Phila-
delphians were deeply shocked on April i7 of last year when Dr.
Walter Judd, Medical Missionary from China, described America's
role in this conflict as that of armorer for Japan. Again Dr. Judd
is bringing this message to the citizens in our City.
The movement for a stoppage of the sale of war supplies
to the Japanese military machine has grown through the United
States during the past year. Very instrumental in this growth has
been the work of the American Committee for Non-Participation in
Japanese Aggression. It has succeededin winning the confidence and
support of leading citizens in this country and of national organi-
zations.
I have been asked by this Committee if I would address to
you this appeal for your generous support of its work. I am glad
to do this because I feel that, in all humanity and justice, our
aid to the Japanese military should be brought to an end, and that,
before this can be achieved, the American public must be widely and
accurately informed. This Committee is attempting to do this free
from hysteria and malice, and deserves the strong support of us all
during the months immediately ahead.
The Executive Director of the American Committee for Non-
Participation in Japanese Aggression is Harry Price, at 8 West 40th
Street, New York City.
Sincerely yours,
Winny S, ahuhe

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.98)
The American Committee
For Non—Participation in Japanese Aggression
8 WEST 40TH STREET, NEW YORK, N. Y.
Henry L. Stimson
LOngacre 5-3062
Cable: NOPARTI
Roger S. Greene
Chairman
Honorary Chairman
Josephine Schain
A. Lawrence Lowell
Vice-Chairman
January 24, 1939.
Mrs. Sidney D. Gamble
Robert E. Speer
Treasurer
William Allen White
Harry B. Price
Honorary Vice-Chairmen
Executive Secretary
America is indignant at the aggressive war in China with its cruelties
to helpless civilians, but Americans -- through lack of knowledge or indif-
ference -- go on ignoring the fact we are supplying the Japanese aggressors
with the sinews of war: scrap iron, oil, steel, trucks -- all more necessary
to her than manufactured munitions.
With one hand we supply the essential materials to the Japanese mili-
tary machine, giving it mobility with trucks and fuel, and allowing our scrap
iron and machinery to be translated into death-dealing armaments. With our
other hand we spend billions for armaments which will protect us against the
mounting threat of aggressor nations. It does not make sense.
President Roosevelt, addressing Congress, has forcibly stated the case
for democracies. He points out that they "cannot forever let pass without
effective protest acts of aggression against sister nations -- acts which
automatically undermine all of us." And he has pointed to methods not involv-
ing use of arms to fight aggression.
This Committee, supported by the counsel of such eminent leaders and
students of foreign affairs as former Secretary of State Stimson, A. Lawrence
Lowell, President Emeritus of Harvard, Robert E. Speer, former President of
the Federal Council of Churches, and William Allen White, distinguished editor,
is organized to oppose aggression with these other peacefu! methods. It pro-
poses a program -- to inform the American public of the part we are playing
in Japanese aggression, and to translate American disapproval into action for
stopping this aid to Japanese militarism.
A minimum of 50,00o is needed to wage an immediate nation-wide camp-
aign against further American participation in Japanese aggression. Careful
plans have been made for the use of such media as newspapers, magazines,
pamphlets, radio, speakers. You will agree that such a sum is small for the
carrying out of so large and urgent an objective.
Your gift, sent today, whether it be for one - ten - fifty dollars or
more, will help stimulate the growth of a demand for intelligent legislation to
"STOP WAR SUPPLIES FOR JAPAN." Such action is overdue, but there is yet time
to move for a reestablishment of international justice and peace in the Pacific,
and for the ultimate preservation of our own democracy.
Sincerely yours
ogr. neene
101
RSG:fg
Chairman

A LOOK AHEAD
The effort, within a few months. to place before the American people a great question of foreign policy for
consideration and decision is no small undertaking. Responses to the Committee's efforts have exceeded early expectations.
The greater task, however, still lies ahead. The question of American support to Japanese aggression will have its im-
portant place in forthcoming discussions of American foreign policy in Washington and decisions that will follow.
The Committee has plans for enlarging every aspect of its program, especially the publicity that must be given to
essential facts as viewed from the standpoint of constructive American policy. A new booklet which presents system-
atically the broad question of American Far Eastern policy at the present time is ready for publication. New leaflets
with brief, vivid presentations are also prepared.
These and other aspects of the Committee's program for the immediate future will depend for their scope and effec-
tiveness upon further contributions from public spirited citizens who are ready to take their part in this far reaching
effort toward the ending of war and the reestablishment of international confidence and cooperation.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF CONTROLS
MEMORANDUM
PARTMENT
October 31,1939
RECEIVED
894.24
V
C
FB:
m
Mr. Harry B. Price, of the American Committee for
Non-Participation in Japanese Aggression, called at my
office by appointment this morning. He said that he had
894.24/728
three questions to take up with me and he proceeded to
take them up seriatim.
(1) He outlined possible restrictions on trade with
Japan which his organization might endeavor to persuade
Congress to authorize and he asked me to comment on these
various proposals from a purely administrative point
of view. Restricting my comments to explaining various
administrative procedures which might be used and various
administrative difficulties which might arise, I gave him
V 1939
the information he desired.
(2) He said that his organization had been informed
that David Warren Ryder was receiving an 1ndirect subsidy
from the Japanese Government in the form of a large mumber
of subscriptions for his publication--a number of subsorip-
tions sufficient to pay all expenses. He asked whether
Mr. Ryder was registered under the act requiring the regis-
tration of agents of foreign principals. I informed him
that

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
that Mr. Ryder was not so registered. I added that we had
had some correspondence with Mr. Ryder and I told him in
confidence that the Department of Justice, at the instance
of the Department of State, was investigating Mr. Ryder's
activities with a view to determining whether or not his
failure to register constituted a violation of the law.
(3) Mr. Price explained that although his organization
solicited funds from American citizens only, nevertheless
it had occasionally received small contributions from
individual Chinese resident in or traveling in the United
States. He asked whether the receipt of such contributions
made it incumbent upon the organization to register under
the act requiring the registration of agents of foreign
principals. I replied in the negative.
Mr. Price handed me the attacned letter of October 30,
with its numerous enclosures. He said that I had already
answered the questions raised in that letter and that no
written reply would be necessary.
Joseph C. Green

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.97)
The New Deal Goose Step
By Carl H. Mote—(Daniel Ryerson Inc., 155 East 39th Street, New York
City. 1939. 248 pp., preface ad index. $2.50. All book stores.)
Some Preliminary Reviews
"The book itself is keen, sedate and convineing . . . Out of the words
and activities of an elaborately authenticated New Dealism, he
makes a revelation of the situation at Washington which is astounding ...
It presents with peculiar realism the contribution which Jewish leaders
have made to Communism . . . While Mr. Mote analyzes frankly every
phase of the New Deal, his writing is free from malice. He sets the record
forth from current sources with the subtlety which belongs to understate-
ment." Former Senator Henry J. Allen of Kansas in an editorial in the
Topeka Daily State Journal, October 4, 1939.
"This book bears evidence of real genius. There have been many
books condemning New Dealism, communism and other 'Tiberal

ownership of property.' "—R. G. Leeds in an editorial in the Richmond

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
THE UNIVERSITY OF TEXAS
Health Service
Austin
November 20, 1939
The Honorable Lyndon Johnson
House of Representatives
Austin, Texas
Dear Sir:
The widespread consideration which is being given to
our foreign policy at this time has h lped me to see even
more clearly the important position which this country
occupies among the nations of the world. I know that you
have given much thought to our foreign policy in the past,
and that you probably will give even more consideration to
it in the future. This is my bit of opinion which I
tender for your consideration.
For several years we have continued to furnish most
of the supplles needed by Japan to prosecute her war against
China. Now that most of Europe 1s engaged in the European
war, our position has become all the more commanding.
Japan is absolutely dependent on this country for her war
supplies, and without our help, she certainly could not
continue her war for many months. I believe that we should
shut off all shipments of supplies to Japan and, 1f neces-
sary, boycott her silk, which is the lifeblood of her foreign
trade - and of which this country buys about 80%. Thus we
could bring about the end of the war on China and end the
Japanese menace on the Pacific. With that threat removed,
England and France could afford to concentrate their entire
strength in Europe and should be able to bring Germany to
terms. This could all be done with practically no risk to
the United States, and it seems to me that such a course on
our part would constitute a real contribution to world peace.
In the months that are ahead, may I, as one of your most
ardent constituents, beg your serious consideration of this
modification of our present foreign policy and hope for the
weight of your support against the imperialistic aims of
Japan and Germany?
Sincerely yours
CAROLINE CROWELL, M.D.
CC/ed

AIR MAIL
The Direotor
Division of Territories and Island Possessions

Washington, D. C.
s 1 r :
This office has received the following information concerning
the organizations which signed the letter transmitting the petition
mentioned in our radio to you yesterdays
(1) Friends of China. This organization opened its
national headquarters at 5o2 Estero Cegado, corner of
P. Paterno, on the third floor, Manila, on December 2,
l937. The "Friends of China" is a society of Filipinos
and foreign residents who feel that China today is the
victim of unlawful Japanese militaristic aggression.
This group has pledged itself to assist China by every
legitimate means, principally through an educational
campaign among Filipinos and foreigners. The group
weloomes all donations for medical supplies, to be
furnisned to wounded Chinese combatants and non-com-
batants. Further activity will be the promotion of a
boycott campaign against Japanese merchandise.
"The Friends of China" point out that the fate of
the Philippines is closely bound up with the fate of
China. A vanquished and slaved China, it believes, would
leave the ambitious Japanese ready to make their move--
against the Philippines. a viotorious free China would
result in the defeat of the militaristic clique in Japan,
and the institution of a democratic, peace-loving govern-
ment in that country. This would remove the dark cloud
of fear which now casts its pall over the Philippines.
(2) Chinese Y.M.C.A. The activities of this organ-
ization seem to be those of the usual Y.M.C.A. 's.
(3) League for the Defense of Demooracy. This
sooiety was organized about 3-1/2 years ago. Its prin-
oipal reason for being seems to be to devote itself to
anti-Japanese activities. The League has an official
publication called the "Vanguard." In July, 1939,
Direotor of Posts Juan Ruiz served notice to the "Van-
guard" that its second-class postal privileges may be
withdrawn if it should again publish articles urging a

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
boyeott of Japanese goods. The League protested the actien of the
Direetor of Posts, deolaring that "He is attempting to squelch the
rights of citizens to express themselves freely and judiciously on
such important and vital probloms affeeting the future of their
liberties and the destiny of their country, which they will be
called upon to defend when the need arises."
(4) Young Philippines. The organisation known as "Young
Philippines" was launched formally on January 7, 1934, with
professional men and students as -embers, under the tutelage of
Roprosentative anuel Roxas. At the organization meetin, the
Fascist salute was adopted, although it claims to be non-partisan.
It has strong PRo tendencies. The orga ization aims to eliminate
older politicians, whom it alleges to be responsi le for claos in
the Philippine Islands oday. Wenceslao inzon is one of the
organizers. Vinzon was the youngest delegate to the constitutional
convention, and organised the recetion for the Delegates. Although
the names that appeared on the ladies committee and reception line
wore representative Filipino names, this only meant that these
representative Filipinos were going to attend"another party."
During the late summer of 1939, there appears to have been a
split in the "Youns Philippines" party. A reetinr mas held on
July 31, 1939, which was presided over by Miss Carmen Planas,
councillor, and wmas headed by eronimo Santiago, Miguel Carnejo,
and Attorney Jose Topacio Nueno. After electing Wenceslao Vinzon
 president, Attorney Arturo Tolentino, vice-president, Gregorio
Acis, goneral secretary of the "Young Philippines," it was decided to
continue said association as a political organisation and approved
the following resolutions:
1. To oppose Bi-Cmoralism.
2. To opiose re-election of President Gueson.
3. Revision of the tax code.
4. To oppose permanent retention of the Philip-
pines by the United States.
Shortly after the meeting mentioned above, Dofia Rosa Sevilla
de Alvero, preminent educator and one of the outstanding women
leaders ef the country, resigned from the party. Her resignation
was followed a few days later by t at of her son.
(5) Congress for Democracy and Collective Security.
This society was apparently organised about a year ago,
and is a branch of the League for the Defense of Democracy,
from which it sprouted. It has a handful of duly regis-

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
tered members, and is sponsoring a boycott against Japan-
ese products. The Japanese in the Philippines have been
very active in attempting to squelch the society. At a
meeting held by the society in the Manila Grand Opera
House, approximately six months'ago, members gave vent
to their feelings in criticizing Japan. Such posters
as "Don't Buy Japanese" were strung about the hall. The
meeting got into trouble with the police, and its chair-
man, Antonio Bautista, who was also head of the Civil
Liberties Union at that time, was placed under arrest
on a warrant is sued by the Mayor of Manila. The party
protested that the Mayor of Manila was gagging freedom
of peech, and fought the charge of the mayor that the
society was violating the neutrality proclamation issued
by President Quezon.
The controversy between the mayor and the chairman
of the society got as far as Malaoeñan, when President
Quezon caused a letter to be directed to Doctor Bautista
indioating the President's displeasure. The President's
letter said that "Condemning a friendly power, and urging
a mass boyoott of its goods causes you and your confreres
to infringe on a rule of conduct which should govern
relations between the Philippines and a friendly nation."
With reference to (3) and (5), I am informed that neither the

November 21, 1939
The Honorable Vic Donahey
Senator from Ohio
House Office Building
Washington; D. C.
Dear Sir:
Being interested in the Chino-Japanese situation and
having heard talks and discussed the problem, there are some
questions which are perplexing me. You, I thought, being
our senior senator, could answer some of them.
Primarily, I have heard that the reason the Arms
Embargo cannot go into effect in the case of Japan and
China is because there 1s no declared "state of war". Why
hasn't the President found a "state of war" existing between
these two countries, which are known quite definitely to be
fighting against each other?
Secondly, I have read that we send Japan fifty-six
per cent of their war materials. If we would stop this
supply, would it hurt our munitions manufacturers' busi-
ness? Would 1t be possible to stop this supply? Would
1t affect the Japanese to such an extent that they could
not advance or make gains on the Chinese?
Is there any seasonable chance of China's winning
the war, say, by extensive guerrilla warfare? If not,
would Japan shut the "Open Door" on the United States and
also try to extend her dominion over the Pacific?
Lastly, the "Cash and Carry" plan, aiding Japan more
than China, is contradictory to the American feelings. What
can we do about it?
I should greatly appreciate any help that you can
give me.
Sincerely yours,
FRED MOREY

Dear sir,
Last Monday, our school was privledged to hear a
very interesting speaker on the Sino-Japanese situation.
Being a former newspaper correspondent in the Orient, he
was very well informed about certain facts. His talk
caused much comment and discussion by many of our
students. As an outcome of this discussion, many
questions were raised by several students.
One of the prevalent questions in the minds of many
students 1s: "Why has not the president declared that a
state of war exists between China and Japan?" If what
we have heard from various speakers 1s true, and if there
1s a fraction of truth in what we read in the newspapers
and magazines, then we cannot understand how this conflict
could be termed anything but a war.
If you can give us any information on this subject,
I am certain that we shall deeply appreciate it.
Respectfully yours,
EDWARD A. EISELE, JR.

Mr. Elmer Thomas:
U. S. Senator
Washington D.C:
Dear Mr. Thomas:
I have been contemplating writing you for sometime,
so now since the spirits have come upon me, "Here it is".
I have been wondering for several years, what is the
matter with the 9 power treaty regarding Japan. Can you
enlighten me es to why or who is to blame for the situation
in China? We are nermitting hundreds of thousands of tons of
scrap and junk iron to be shipped to Japan also cotton,
which is nothing else but war material. ("raw") They are
tearing down our only market we have in the East. And if
we still permit this and all other insults that Japan
has caused (thfs) country, in a few years we will be
eliminated entirely from China. And furthermore if the
Open Door is closed to our country, then we will not only
have the 10 million unemoloyed, but it will be doubled as
many more times the later.
Mr. Thomas will you kindly enlighten me on this
subject, or send me some datta to where I can think clearly
and talk intelligently. We can't sit idly by and allow
our country's "rights" to be trampled on. I served over
seas in the last war. We went "blindfolded" in the conflict.
So we surely don't want war again we must bring the Japanese
to their senses and have an embargo that will make her keep
her word.
Hoping you can see my attitude
Yours sincerely
BEN F. MEHR
RFD #8 Box 94A
Okla City
Okla.

December 7, 1939.

DL.MENT OF STATE
