Co- Mr. Green:
EA:
FE8
Messrs. Edward B. Wilber and Arthur P. Hall of the
Aluminum Company of Americe, 605 Southern Building, wash-
111.942
ington, n. C., came in to see me this afternoon in regard
894.24/741
to the exportation of aluminum ores to Japan.
Mr. Wilber stated that his company was familiar with
the Secretary's policy of discouraging the sale of airplanes
and aeronautical equipment for export to countries the
armed forces of which are making use of airplanes for attack
upon civilian populations. He inquired whether a similar
moral embargo had been imposed on the exportation of com-
modities such as aluminum ores to Japan. I replied in the
negative.
Mr. Wilber then inquired whether the credit provisions
of the Neutrality Act apply to Japan as a belligerent. I
7
replied to the effect that Japan has not been named as a
belligerent under the Neutrality Act and that the provisions
of Section 7 thereof are not, therefore, applicable to Japan.
He

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
He then made inquiry as to the position of Japan, as con-
trasted with other neutrals, in regard to the extension
of credit. I replied that Japan is in the same position
as all other neutrals, namely, that such generul laws, as
for example the Johnson Act, as might be applicable in a
given case, would apply. I added that the Department was
not in a position, however, to encourage the extension of
credit to Japanese interests. I made clear that this state-
ment was merely a remark of mine and should not be construed
as an announced policy of the Department, since the Secre-
tary of State has not, to my knowledge, made any announce-
ment on the subject. Mr. Wilber stated that the aluminum
ores about which he inquired might be used in Japan for
any one of a thousand purposes. He indicated, prior to
departure, that his inquiries were largely theoretical
since, as a matter of fact, his company has not extended
credit and does not plan to extend any credit in connec-
tion with export sales to Japan.
Inquiry was also made as to the status of trade be-
tween this country and Japan following the termination of
our commercial treaty with that country. I stated that,
in the absence of a treaty, our commercial relations with
the
Japan would be governed in each country by/domestic laws
and

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
and regulations of that country subject, of course, to
such provisions of international law as might be applicable.
e
S
Edgar P. Allen
Co:EPA:HJG

be allowed to rest where it was. In that case the companies
which had already been informed through their renresentatives
that this Government would not look with favor upon the sale
of

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.99)
-9~
of information in regard to the processes for the manu-
facture of high-test aviation gasoline could decide (a)
whether they wished to abide by the policy of the Govern-
ment, and (b) whether any particular transaction would in
fact contravene that policy; (:) the comoanies having been
informed of the policy of this Government could, if they so
desired, request the Depertment's opinion as to whether any
individual transaction would contravene that policy. If
this request were made in writing, vith a full statement
of the facts, it would be transmitted to the ar and Navy
Departments for their consideration, anc the Denartment
would endeavor, on the basis of the findings of those two
Deoartments, to reoly to the question asked.
Mr. Badger and others would not agree to the first
orocedure. Mr. Hovard and Mr. Griffith would not agree to
the second procedure.
Mr. Badger stated that to agree to the first procedure
would oblige his company either to refrain from entering
into a transaction to which it was committed and which it
was convinced would not in fact be of any significent
adventage to the Jaoanese in connection with the production
of high-test aviation gasoline, or to be put in the position
of apoearing to contravene the exoressed policy of the Govern-
ment. Mr. Howard insisted that to follow the second procedure
would result in complete confusion; that neither the War Depart-
ment

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
ment nor the Navy Department had experts comoetent to pass
upon the technical problems which would be involved in
the various proposed transactions which the companies would
submit to the Denartment nd that the only means of dealing
with this question was ror sll o" the comoanies to rgree
not to sell to Jananese interests any further information
relating to the production of hign-test viotion masoline.
The ooints of view of the two gentlemen were obviously
irreconcilable nd it seemed likely thot further discussion
between the reoresentatives of competing interests would
degenerate into an acrimonious dispute.
I therefore asked
whether anyone had any third oroposal to make.
After a period of silence several of the gentlemen
present concurred in the oninion that agreement among them
was impossible. Mr. Badger, for his part, said that he
intended to submit to the Denartment for full consideration
the project which his company had in mind, and that if after
consideration of that project the Department were to inform
him that it would appear to contravene the general policy
which I had stated, his company would refrain from oroceed-
ing with the project provided that it could be authorized
to inform the Jananese of the reason why it had broken off
negotiations.
Mr. Howard and Mr. Griffith left my office. The others
followed after an interval long enough to enable Mr. Howard
and Mr. Griffith to be well out of the way.

Mr. Joseph C. Green, Chief,

Dear Mr. Green:
α
394.24/743
I have reported to my associates the outcome of our
discussion in your office.
We regret that unforeseen objections on the part of
some of the parties appear to have prevented immediate unanimous
agreement to the broad principle of abstaining from the sale of any
technique for the production of aviation gasoline at this time to
the country in question. Without unanimous agreement we fear it is
not practical to do anything effective, because of the adverse result,
not only on the absolute earnings, but on the relative competitive
positions of all of the interests and groups involved. As appeared
through our discussions, in addition to the principal parties who
have the most important interests in the processes in question, there
are many other parties who are interested in them in a less important
way, but whose rights must be protected if the situation remains a
competitive one.
The attitude of our own organization remains unchanged.
We recognize that in connection with the country in question our
Government has very grave problems on its hands and we are ready to
cooperate. We do not see however how we can with any propriety attempt
A
to advise other American interests who are as well informed as our-
selves as to their duty. The only constructive thing that we see we
can do to assist you at this moment is to supply you with a memorandum
giving a brief complete statement of the entire technical position.
This may be useful in the further consideration of the matter by the
interested parties of the three departments.
We remain at your service.
Very truly yours,
743
Fraul- A /toward

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.99)
MEMORANDUM ON AVIATION GASOLINE
November 27, 1939
The critical specification for aviation gasoline suitable for military
use is its octane number. The higher the octane number, the greater the power which
can be developed for the same engine with it, and the less the consumption of gaso-
line per horse power hour. Modern military engines require a minimum octane number
of about 87 and a maximum of about l00. To get the maximum benefit from the higher
octane fuels, the engine must be designed for them; but a very large part of the
value of hirher octane mumbers can be obtaine by minor changes or even by mere ad-
justments of engines designed for lower octane number fuel. Yore than any other
single factor, perhaps, t e octane number of the fuel available is the one which
limits the performance charact ristics o militar: ircraft. It is only the fear
of the goverrments concerned that adequate supplies would not be available that
has prevented them from at all standardizing on en ines designed for operation
on l00 octane fuel for the last two years.
The normal and principel source of su: l) of aviation gasoline is
selected crude oil, the principal production of which is in the Gulf Coast of the
U.S., California, Venezuela, Peru, Borneo, Roumania and Russia. The highest prac-
tical standard for these natural supplies is bout 74 octane nunber, although some
of the Borneo product is as good as 77 octane number. The total supplies of the
natural product of this quality are very definitely limited and are far less than
might conceivably be required. Even at the present time the market is being supplied
very largely by a synthetic product, although there is doubtless a substantial quan-
tity of natural product not being used for various commercial reasons.
The sources of supply of synthetic aviation gasoline are as follows!
(1) Hydrogenation. This is the method used universa ly in Germany, and
to a very large extent in the U.S. The product produced is of excellent quality
up to the level of the very best Borneo natural spirit, being about 75 to 77 octane
number. The hydrogenation process is controlled, for the U.S., by Hydro Patents
Company, a corporation of which Standard Oil Company (N. J.) owns about 40%, the
remaining 60% being held by 17 other American oil companies. For Germany the
process is controlled by the I.G. Farbenindustrie, A.G. Outside of the U.S. and
Germany, the process is controlled by International Hydrogenation Patents Company,
a Dutch Corporation owned by Standard Oil Company (N.J.) and the Royal Dutch Shell
group, the German I.G. company retaining a certain royalty interest. The raw
material for gasoline manufactured by hydrogenation is, preferably, selected light
gas oil; but either kerosene or fuel oil could be employed, with less satisfactory
results.
(2) Catalytic Cracking. The raw materials for this process would be
about the same as in the case of hydrogenation and the quality about the same, but
the yield considerably smaller. The first catalytic cracking process in commercial
use is that of the Houdry Process Corporation of Philadelphia, of which 1/3 interest
belongs to Sun Oil Company, 1/3 to Socony-Vacuum Oil Co., Inc. and 1/3 to the inventor,
Houdry, and to other individuals including the French groups who originally financed
him. The French patent rights are independently held by the original Houdry company
of France. A second catalytic cracking process for producing aviation gasoline was
developed by a group consisting of Standard Oil Co. (N.J.), I.G. Farbenindustrie,A.G.,

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Anglo-Iranian Oil Co., Ltd., The M. W. Kellogg Company, Standard Oil Co. (Indiana);
and further developments of this same process are inv lved in discussions between
the parties above named and the Royal Dutch Shell group, The Texas Company, and the
Universal Oil Products Co., which latter is an American development and licensing
corporation controlled by Royal Dutch Shell and Standard Oil Co. of California.
In addition, its securities are held by Standard Oil Co. (N.J.), The Texas Company.
Standard Oil Co. (Indiana), Gulf Refining Company and Atlantic Refining Co.
(3) Hydroforming. This is closely related to the Hydrogenation Process,
but is much simpler. It operates on or inary motor gasoline or similar distillates
as a charging stock and produces from t is material about 65% of aviation gasoline
of a quality equal to the c talytically cracked or nydrogenated product. The domi-
nant interest in this process at the present time is in Standerd Oil Co. (N.J.) but
in view of its relation to other developments and earlier contracts it should be
said also to involve the interests of the Royal Dutch Shell, Standard Oil Co. (Indiana).
Anglo-Iranian Oil Co., Ltd., and the M. W. Kellogg Co.
Aviation gasoline of the quality of 74 to 77 octane number mey therefore
be made from crude oil, end by the threr types of synthetic processes mentioned. To
raise the octane level of this gasoline to the range of 87 to 1co required for use
in modern aviation engines, two expedients are employed (1) the addition of tetra-
ethy1 lead and (2) the addition of blendin agents.
TETRAETHYL LEAD. Tetraethy1 lead is manufactured in the U.S., England,
France and Germany. There is a small pirate production in Italy, and a reputed
pirate production also just oing into operation in Mexico. Tetraethy1 lead and
its processes of manufacture are controlled in the U.S. by General Motors and
Stan ard Oil Co. (N.J.); and outside of the U.S. b. these same companies, the Royal
Dutch Shell, Anglo-Iranian, Socony-Vacuum, Texas and Standard Oil Co. of California.
The exact limits of usefulness of lead in raising the octane number of
gasoline are still a subject of technical disnute. It is the sole means employed
universally to make the base 87 grade from the naturally available and synthetic
stocks of 74 to 77. In larger proportions than is acceptable to some engine builders
and military aviation services, it can be used to raise octane levels as high as 96,
without blending agents of any kind. The importance of octane number increases as
the level rises, however, so that there is far more difference between 96 octane
number and 100, than between 74 and 78, or between 87 and 91.
BLENDING AGENTS. The blending agents used to improve the octane number
of aviation gasoline are as follows:
(1) Iso-Octane itself. The processes for its manufacture are controlled
by Standard Oil Co. (N.J.) and Royal Dutch Shell throughout the world, but Universal
Oil Products Co. has certain limited agency rights. Iso-octane is produced from
isobutylene gas, which is first polymerized and then hydrogenated. Isobutylene gas
can be made available in substantial quantities up to this time only at very large
refining centers, or adjacent to certain crude fields. The total commercially avail-
able supply is inadequate to demand for materials of this character. Sixty to
seventy percent of good quality aviation gasoline of 74 to 77 octane number, with
30 to 40% of iso-octane and an addition of 3 cc. of lead per gallon, is the standard
formula for first-quality l00 octane aviation gasoline.

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
(2) The next material for blending is a polymerization product of isobuty-
lene with normal butylene, which requires also to be hydrogenated. This process is
controlled by Universal Oil Products, Royal Dutch Shell and Standard Oil Co. (N.J.)
throughout the world. The product is not quite so good as pure iso-octane, but the
amount which can be produced is materially larger.
(3) A third blending stock for aviation gasoline is the polymer referred
to as "Neohexane" by the Phillips Petroleum Company. Like iso-octane and the copolymer
of iso-octane and isobutylene it is made primarily from the butanes obtained as a by-
product of crackins operations, or fron tie oil fields. Ri hts to this process are
controlled b. t e Pii lips c mpany  b tre Polymerization Process Corporation,
owned by P. I ips, Staadurd Oil C. (N..), The Texas Co., Standard Oil Co. (Indiana)
an The . W.KelloCo.
(4) Another tlendin agsent of a different cheracter is isopropyl ether,
made by the treatm nt of propylene gas. This moterial is somewhat more effective
than iso-octane itself for blendin purposes, althou of slightly lower calorific
value. The patents on this roduct for aviation fuel purposes are controllod by
Standard Cil Co. (N.t.), but the tee nique of te process is also known to the Royal
Dutcn Shell roup and to the Carbide and Carbon Chemicals Corporation, both of whom
produce this mat rial as a by-product. Potential supplies of isopropyl ether in
most refining centers wouli be lar r than of any other blendin: product except pos-
sibly aviation alkylate. referred to below.
(5) The last and much the most important of the blending agents is
"Aviation Alkylate." It is produced primarily from butanes but ma. include also
propane and pentane. The cost is much lower, and the quantity which can be produced
in a given refinery from a given supply of hydrocarbon gas much reat r than in the
case of other blendin agents. T alkylation process is, in fact, the one method
which seems to guarantee at the present time thot the supply of loo octane aviation
motor fuel may be cqual to he pr sent foreseeable demand. The alkylation process
is controlled by Standard Oil Co. (N.J.), Royal Dutch Shell, An lo-Iranian and Texas.
The M. W. Kellog? Co. is acting as agent, as is the Universal Oil Products Co.
The world's present supply of aviation gasoline of the quality required
for military purposes, i.e. 37 to 100 octane nwnber, is therefore absolutely dependent
upon the use of a variety of processes for production of the basic aviation gasoline
itself, of suitable high qualit, and of the addition agent lead, and the blending
agents--which are, in fact, all polymers of gaseous hydrocarbons. The supply of
aviation gasoline available to any country depends on two factors: its access to the
necessary raw materials and its knowledge of the highly specialized technique.
The processes mentioned re all in commercial use, partly competitive and
partly supplementary and complementary. Only in the U.S. is there available at this
time full t echnical information on all of these processes.
PROCESSES ALREADY AVAILABLE TO THE COUNTRY IN QUESTION.
The country in question could naturally produce aviation gasoline by
straight distillation, providing it could find sufficient supplies of the required
high-quality crude oils. For economic reasons such supplies are certainly not avail-
able at the present time. The country does not yet possess the full technique for

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.96)
-4
any of the synthetic processes of producing aviation gasoline. It has made
repeated efforts to pirate the Hydrogenation Process, but has not been com-
pletely successful. It has no technique as yet on the catalytic cracking or
ydroforming processes.
As concerns blending agents, it has no technique on the manufac-
ture of tetraethyl lead. It has the technique on the mamufacture of iso-octane,
and the sut stitute : roduct seleetive p o'ymer, ineludin the sinple hydrogena-
tion require! o fit these produets for use. This techrique was acquired from
the Universal Oil Products Co. within the last year. It has no technique on
an of the other blending agent processes.
FRAN: A HOWARD

would be approximately 4500 barrels per day. To convert this
production to avietion fuel (loo octane) would necessitate the
daily purchase or manufecture of pproximately 715,000 c.c.
of tetraethyl lead and spproximately 22oo barrels of iso-octene.
Therefore, assuming the crude was available end the blending
fluids could be obtained, the potential aviation fuel is 6700
barrels per dey.
Potentiel Iso-Octene Production
The best source of raw material (butane unsaturates)
for 1so-octane production 1s fixed gases from thermel cracking
units. The potential production of iso-octane, using all
butane unsaturetes produced by all Japanese refineries, is of
the order of 450 barrels per dey. Two iso-octane plants
licensed and designed by en American compery are now being
erected in Japen. The combined capacity of the two plants is
reported as 20o barrels of iso-octane per dey. Utilization of
the ultimate production would produce, es loo octane aviation
fuel and assuming tetraethyl lead available, approximately
looo barrels per dey. The plants now under construction, on
the basis of the same assumptions, will provide 450 to 500
barrels per day.
Tetreethyl Lead Production
Beyond the statement made during our discussion by
Mr. Frank Howard, no first hand information is available on
this point.
Hydrogenation
One refinery in Jepan is operating its own hydro-
cracking process, and it is understood that negotiations are
being conducted, or have been successfully concluded, with
the German firm of I.G. Farben Industrie, A.G., for rights
under hydrogenation patents owned by that Company.
The Houdry Process
The Houdry Process is catalytic. The catalyst used
is manufactured by Catalytic Development Company (a subsidiary
of Houdry Process Corporetion), and the license egreement with
the Japanese group contains no provisions for divulging the
method of manufecture or the in redients of any catelyst.
For producing a base aviation gasoline, the Houdry
Process charging stock is a naphtha having an A.S.T.M. boiling

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
E.B. BADGER & SONS CO.
-3-
The Honorable The Secretary of State
December 6, 1939.
range of 325oF. I.B.P. to 4500F. E.P., or a ges oil of 440oF.
I.B.P. to 725oF. E.P. The base aviation gasoline yield from
naphtha charge will be approximately 50% and from gas o1l 33%,
depending upon the finel specificetions desired.
Quality of Houdry Base Aviation Gasoline
The Houdry Process produces a base aviation fuel
and not a finished aviation gasoline. The product is com-
perable with e base aviation gasoline distiiled from a
selected crude, in that it must be blended with iso-octane
or other high octane hydrocerbon and tetraethyl lead to pro-
duce a finished 1oo octane product. The use, by the Japanese,
of their contemplated iso-octane production of 2oo barrels
per day would provide, with Houdry base stock, e smaller
amount of 100 octane gasoline than the 450 to 500 barrels
they could produce with a natural product from e selected
crude.
The Houdry bese stock may be used for producing a
lower grade of aviation fuel (86 to 90 octane) by using 3 to
4 c.c. of tetraethyl lead per gallon. The octane ppreciation
by the use of lead quantities above 4 c.c. is slight. As
regards these points, the seme results cen be obtained with a
base aviation gasoline from a selected crude.
Financial Aspects
The license fee and the estimated cost for engineer-
ing, material, etc. for the initial instelletion, which is now
being negotiated, involves an expenditure of approximately
$5,750,000, and the greater part of which is to be placed in
escrow in an Americen Bank. Payments are to be made by the
escrow agent at stated intervals, and the final payment on the
license is to be made 18 months from the date of signing the
license agreement. Of this money, approximately $1,500,000.
will be spent in the United States, in addition to the License
fee, for steel products, mechinery, instruments, engineering,
etc.
Conclusions
From the above, the following conclusions are
logical:
(1) The independent production of evietion fuel from
petroleum by the Japanese 1s only possible by
their obtaining a source for:

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.98)
E.B. BADGER & SONS CO.
-4
The Honorable The Secretary of State
December 6, 1939.
(a) Crude oil having an appreciable content
of high octane gasoline.
(b) Raw materiels for the manufacture of iso-
octane or other high octane hydrocarbons
for blending, or e process for producing
such rew meterial.
(c) Knowledge and means for the manufacture
of tetraethyl lead.
(2) Installation of the Houdry Process will not
greatly further their plans for independence as:
(a) They will still be dependent upon factors
(la) and (1b), and to an unknown extent
upon (1c).
(b) The control of the catalyst remains in this
country ena eny attempt to overcome the
catalyst dependency will recuire at least
three to four years.
(c) They are dependent upon petroleum fractions
for cherging stock to the process.
(3) The financial aspects of the transaction will:
(a) Tend to further weaken an adverse trade
balence.
(b) Provide thousands of man hours work for
American industry.
(c) Through the direct profit derived, financially
strengthen an American research organization,
engineers end manufecturers.
For the above reesons it is believed the negotiations
with the Japanese should be concluded, if possible.

Houdry Process Negotiations:
Negotiations have been under wey for over one year

Universal Oil Products Negotiations:
From the best knowledge available, it is believed
U.o.P. has completed negotiations giving all patent rights on
polymerization, catelytic cracking, etc. to a Japanese Cor-
poration. Also, it is rumored U.O.P. now actively working
with Nippon Sode (Nisson) for catalytic cracking unit end
upon satisfectory demonstration of pilot plant performance,
plant is to be designed by Universal Oil Products and material,
etc. to be handled by a Japanese Trading Corporation.
Univeraal Oil Products have recently licensed two
Japanese companies for iso-octane units which were designed
by U.O.P. and are now under construction.
Hydrogenation:
Negotiations are now under way, or ere completed,
for purchase of patents on hydrogenation direct from I.G.
Ferben Industrie A.G., Germany. It is not known why these
negotiations were handled direct, as it was generelly thought
that the American I.G. Chemical Corporation hnd the world-
wide licensing rights on hydrogenation of petroleum.
M. W. Kellogg Company:
A Japanese group under the tutelage of Asano Bussan,
a trading company, has reteined an American engineer as con-
sultent for negotiations with the Kellogg Company. Status of
these negotiatlons are not known, but it is believed they are
only in the preliminary stage.

December 7, 1939.
PA/H:
Mr. Don Emery, General Counsel of the Phillips
Petroleum Company, called at my office this morning.
I told him that this Government was disturbed at
reports that oil companies in this country were preparing
894.24/750
to sell to Japanese interests information which would
enable the Japanese to produce high-test aviation gaso-
line. I said that we felt that 1t would be very much to
the advantage of the American Government if the Japanese
were for the next few years kept dependent upon purcnases
in the United States for high-test aviation gasoline and
that therefore we hoped that American companies would not
sell to Japanese interests the information to which I had
referred.
Mr. Emery said that he felt certain that his company
would be willing to cooperate fully with the Government
in any policy which might be enunciated provided that its
competitors in this field were al so willing to comoly.
I

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
I told Mr. Emery that we were endeavoring to bring
about cooperation in this matter on the part of all com-
peting companies and that it was possible that we might
call a conference of renresentatives of those companies
in the near future.
Mr. Emery said that, if anything was to be done con-
cerning this matter, it should be done with the least
possible delay as a Japanese mission was already en route
to the United States to negotiate a contract with his
company and as that mission would arrive in less than
two weeks. He said that, if any conference were called,
he or some other representative of his company would be
glad to come from Oklahoma and that, as the company owned
its own planes, which were constantly in use in transport-
ing officers of the company from one part of the country
to the other, the Department need have no hesitancy about
summoning an officer from Bartlesville, Oklahoma.

The meeting lasted for two hours and a half. The
princinal question at issue and a number of subsidiary ques-
tions were fully discussed.
Representatives of several com-
panies furnished important information in regard to their
contracts or contemplated contracts with Japanese interests.
Mr. Berle set forth clearly the position of the Department.
From the discussion, it appeared that the Universal
Oil Products Company had already furnished Japanese inter-
ests with a great deal of information in regard to the
production of high ouality aviation gasoline; that the
Japanese had evidently not as yet obtained all the informa-
tion in regard to this matter which they desired; and that
the Universal Oil Products Company, E. B. Badger and Sons
Company, and the Phillips Petroleum Company are now engaged
1n

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.98)
一3
in negotiations which, if successful, would result in the
transmission of further pertinent information to the Japan-
ese.
. The renresentatives of all of the commanies present
expressed their willingness to coonerate fully with the
Government in carrying out any nolicy which the Department
might formulate. Some of them were very insistent, how-
ever, that it should be understood that this willingness
to cooperate should be considered as contingent upon
complete cooperation by all competing companies. It ap-
peared that, should the Government adhere to its request
that no further information in regard to the manufacture
of high quality aviation gasoline should be transmitted
to Japanese interests, compliance with this request would
entail a greater immediate sacrifice by E. B. Badger and
Sons Company than by any of the other companies. Mr. Badger
suggested that, if the policy expressed by Mr. Berle was to
be adopted, it should be made applicable also to sales to
the U.S.S.R. and perhans to Germany, and, furthermore, that
the policy in respect to the sale of information should be
made equally applicable to the sale of such blending agents
as tetra-ethyl lead, iso-octane, neo-hexane, and alkylates.
At the end of the discussion, the attached memorandum
was drawn up. It was agreed to by the representatives of
all the companies present.

January 3, 1940.

Mr. Secretary:
With reference to Tokyo's 717, Decem-
ber 30, noon, wnich indicates that the Japanese
Ambassador may, under instruction, protest
against the moral embargo laid down in the
Department's statenent of December 20 (and also
in the Department's statement of December 15)
on the ground that such embargo infringes the
Treaty of Commerce and Navigation of 19ll
between the United States and Japan, it is
suggested that if the Japanese mbassador
makes a protest orally a reply be given to him
substantially as follows:
"The position of the Government of
the United States in regard to this subject
is clearly indicated in the statement
released by the President on December 2,
19 9, and in the Department of tate's
press releases of December 15 and Decem-
ber 20, 1939. (Copies of these releases
might be handed to the Jaranese Aabassador).
Conformity by American manufacturers and
exporters to the policy as outlined in
those statements is a voluntary act on
their part. It will be noted that no coun-
try is spooifioally mentioned in the
Department's press releases. The Depart-
ment, in letters in reply to inquiries by
American exporters as to the names of the
countries to which is made alplicable the
policy under reference, has invariably
declined to specify the name of any country
but has stated that the inquirer will have
no

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
no difficulty in ascertaining from the
daily press which countries are engaged
in the unprovoked bombing and machine-
gunning of civilian populations from
the air. With regard to the decision
contained in the Department's press re-
lease of December 20 which relates to
the discontinuance of delivery to cer-
tain countries of plans, manufacturing
rights or technical information re-
quired for the production of high
quality aviation gasoline, this repre-
sents, as stated in the press release,
an extension of the previously announced
policy and is also designed to conserve in
this country certain technical informa-
tion of strategic importance. It is
believed that the need of safeguarding
such information is a point which will
be readily understood by the Japanese
Government.
"In view of the foregoing circum-
stances, this Government is unable to
agree that its action in inviting the
voluntary cooperation of American ex-
porters in conforming to the policy
pursued by the American Government and
the American people of wholeheartedly
condemning the unprovoked bombing and
machine-gunning of civilian populations
from the air constitutes an infringement
of the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation
of 19ll between the United States and
Japan."
Should

Ambassador take the forn of a written com-

course depend upon the contents of that com-
munication.

DIVISION OF CONTROLS

OMMUNICATIONS

E：
2A
PA/H:
Mr. H. J. Halle, President of the Universal Oil Products
Company, called me by telephone from New York this morning.
He said that a representative of Japanese interests had
called at his office earlier in the day to discuss the pos-
sibility of obtaining from the company further technical
894.24/764
information in regard to the production of high-quality
aviation gasoline and that he had read to the Japanese
representative portions of the memorandum enclosed with the
Department's letter of December 19, 1939. He said that
the Japanese representative had asked whether the refusal
of the company to enter into further contracts and to carry
out its existing contract with Japanese interests was due
to a request or an order from the American Government. In
reply, Mr. Halle pointed to the terms of the memorandum and
said that he interpreted the memorandum as an order. The
994124
Japanese representative then, said that he doubted whether
the American Government had any legal authority to issue

Mr. Halle

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-

and suggested that he might dictate a telegram and

Mr. Halle said thet he would prefer to come to

December 19, 1939.
Mr. Halle called at my office this morning accompanied
by his associates, Mr. W.D. Whitney end Mr. Stanley Wilson.
Mr. Henderson of Eu and Mr. Ballantine of FE participated
in the conversation.
Mr. Halle told in great detail and at considerable
length of the negotiation of his company'ε contract with
the U.S.S.R. for the construction of plants for the pro-
duction of high octane gas. What he said was for the most
part

=== Page 470 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
part a repetition of his statements at the conference
between representatives of oil companies and officers
of the Department in my office on December 14. He re-
peated that the carrying out of the policy explained by
Mr. Berle at that conference would necessitate the break-
ing of the contract. He exolained that for that reason
he and his associates wished to see the letter and the
memorandum which were to be sent to the company in ac-
cordance with the understanding reached at the conference.
He reiterated with emohesis what he had said at the con-
ference, viz: that the comoany was prepared in any event
to cooperate fully with the Government in carrying out any
policy which the Department might enunciate.
Mr. Whitney, general counsel for the company, explained
that the breaking of the contract would probably result in
arbitration proceedings in the Soviet Union in which the
company would be condemned to pay heavy damages, perhaps
running into the millions; that eventually a claim might
be made which would become the subject of litigation in
the State of New York; and that the letter and the memoran-
dum might prove to be extremely important documents in
connection with that litigation.
工

=== Page 471 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
I handed Mr. Halle a copy of the letter and the memo-
randum, informing him that they had not as yet been approved
by the Secretary and therefore could not be considered as
official but that I had reason to believe that they would
be sent in substantially the form in which they appeared in
these copies.
Mr. Halle and his associates strongly urged that the
letter and the memorandum should be revised and that, in
accordance with the original understanding reached at the
conference mentioned above, Japan should be mentioned by
name in the memorandum and a separate letter addressed to
the company specifically stating that the policy expressed
in the memorandum was aoplicable to the U.S.S.R., which
they wished also mentioned by name. They expressed strong
objection to the use of "certain countries" in the memorandum
and to the fact that Japan and the U.S.S.R. were dealt with
in the same document. They wished the letter and the memo-
randum revised not only so that they might have in hand
documents which they could show to Japanese or Soviet agents
and which would contain no reference to any country but the
particular country which the agent represented, and they
insisted that the use of "certain countries" instead of
"Japan" and "the U.S.S.R." might make the letter and the
memorandum

=== Page 472 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
memorendum useless as evidence to be presented in defense
of the company if litigation resulted from breach of con-
tract by the company.
I told Mr. Halle and his associates that the Department
could not agree to the revision of the letter and the memo-
randum which they desired. I added, however, that there
were in the files of the Department memoranda indicating
clearly that "certain countries" meant Japan and the U.S.S.R.
and that if, after the lapse of several years, these memo-
randa should be required by the company for its defense in
litigation, certified copies could probably be obtained for
presentation in evidence.
Mr. Halle and his associates then objected to the
letter on the ground that a whole series of comoanies was
listed in the first paragraph and that their company was
last in the list. They said that to show such a letter
to Japanese or Soviet agents would cause the company to
lose face.
I explained that the list was alphabetical.
They asked whether an additional letter might not be
addressed to the company in which the list could be omitted.
After some discussion, it was agreed that, if the com-
pany would address a letter to the Department enclosing
copies

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
copies of the contracts which had been referred to in the
discussion and asking whether the policy which we had been
discussing was applicable to such contracts, the Department
would probably find itself in a position to reply in the
affirmative in a letter in which no other comoanies would
be mentioned.
A long discussion then ensued in regard to the steps
which the company should take to withdraw its engineers
from the U.S.S.R. and the assistance which the Department
might be able to render in that connection.
While the discussion was in progress, Mr. Halle re-
ceived by teleohone a telegram from one of the engineers -
Orion Newell Miller - as follows:
"Going to Moscow on twenty-second by order
from Embassy for passport validation stop Order
states Embassy will then decide whether additional
stay is to be allowed stop"
It was finally decided that the Department would trans-
mit, through the Embassy in Moscow at the company's expense,
a telegram for transmission to the engineers. A rough draft
of this telegram was drawn up. Furthermore, it was decided
that the Department would send another telegram to the
Ambassador explaining the policy under discussion and con-
taining appropriate instructions in regard to the return of
the engineers. After I had consulted Mr. Davis of FA by
telephone

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
telephone, it was agreed that this latter telegram should
authorize the Ambassador to advance funds if additional
funds were necessary to enable the engineers and their
families to return to the United States and that the company
would deposit the necessary funds with the Department if the
Ambassador made any advances.
Mr. Halle then raised the question of possible with-
drawal from Japan of the company's engineers engaged in
constructing plants in that country.
It was made clear to Mr. Halle and his associates that
the company's engineers in Japan were, from the point of
view of possible annoyance or danger, in an entirely dif-
ferent position from the company's engineers in the U.S.S.R.
I said that I did not believe that the Department could at-
tempt to specify in detail just what action each company
must take in specific circumstances to carry out the policy
which had been fully explained by Mr. Berle at the conference
on December 14 but that the specific action in each case
must be left to the judgment of the companies which had
agreed to comply with that policy.
At Mr. Ballantine's request, Mr. Halle repeated at
great length information which he had given at the con-
ference on December 14 in regard to the company's contracts,
past and present, with Japanese interests.
Mr. Halle

MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION WITH MR. JAMES A. MOFFETT,
OF THE TEXAS PETROLEUM CORPORATION
Mr. James A. Moffett called me from New York City
this morning to inquire about the "moral embargo" which
he understood the State Department was placing upon the
894.24/768
sale to the Manchurian Oil Monopoly of certain technical
DINISIN OO
information and aviation gasoline refining machinery.
He said that the information regarding the meeting had
上
reached him through the president of the Shell Oil Com-
pany. I informed Mr. Moffett that I knew nothing of the
matter but would look into it and call him back.
After talking with Mr. Green, I telephoned Mr. Moffett
and informed him that it was true the president of the
Shell Oil Company, together with the representatives of
other oil companies, had met with Mr. Green in a conference
last week and that the Department had requested these com-
panies to withhold any further delivery of plans and tech-
π
N
nical information to Japanese and Soviet interests. The
Department recognized that companies other than those which
were represented at the meeting have an interest in this
policy, and it was, therefore, agreed that those attending
the

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the meeting would take it upon themselves to communicate
the information to the representatives of these other com-
panies.
Mr. Moffett expressed appreciation for this information
and said that he was entirely in accord with the policy
adopted. He stated that his only reason in telephoning
was because he had received the information about the meet-
ing from the president of a non-American oil company. I
assured Mr. Moffett that representatives of a number of
companies, all American, with the exception of Shell Oil
(so far as I knew), were likewise present at the meeting
held in the State Department last week.

the exportation of such blending agents as tetra-ethyl lead,
894.24
iso-octane, neo-hexane, and alkylates to Japan or Russia,
please be advised that we do not manufacture or sell any of
these four products, nor do we expect to be in a position to
either manufacture or sell same,at any rate within a year.
We will accordingly not attempt to make any
reports, but I will endeavor to keep in mind the fact that
if we should at some future date be in a position to make
such sales you would like to have a report on same.
Very truly yours,
RobutMoson
